# Preface.

Reader, you who fulfill your duties toward God, toward the state, toward your neighbor, and toward yourself solely because <term>Christianity</term> <note>original: "Christenthum"</note> commands you to do so: I beg of you, set this book aside at first glance. Do not say: "Ha! You only make me curious; I must read it!" Believe me, you will lose your <term>peace of soul</term> <note>original: "Seelenruhe"; a state of spiritual and mental tranquility</note> over it, and I shall then wash my hands in innocence, for I have told you so beforehand. If you still refuse to take this advice, then read the Appendix as well, and read it again. Study it, and grasp it entirely, if you have the strength for it. Then you will find your peace of soul once more, and you will fulfill all duties toward God, toward the state, toward your neighbor, and toward yourself with double the joy.

I divide the thinking and reading public, in regard to my book, into three classes. To the first, I reckon those who search for truth with an unbiased mind and accept it, whether it has long been recognized or only recently discovered. I say, to this class I reckon all those who set themselves above the <term>prejudice</term> <note>The author refers here to the "Vorurtheil" of tradition—the habit of believing things simply because they are old or widely accepted.</note> that opinions and doctrines should be true merely because they have been considered established truths by many for millennia. To the second, I count those who praise <term>Voltaire</term> <note>François-Marie Arouet (1694–1778), a leading figure of the French Enlightenment known for his criticism of organized religion.</note> and his like as genuine lights of the world, and about things,

<summary>The author opens with a provocative warning that his book may challenge the reader's religious foundation, suggesting that only a thorough study of the appendix can restore one's peace of mind. He begins to categorize his audience, distinguishing between those who seek truth without prejudice and those who follow Enlightenment skeptics like Voltaire.</summary>
<keywords>preface, religious duty, peace of soul, Enlightenment, truth, prejudice, Voltaire, skepticism</keywords>

# Preface.

...things they do not understand, merely laugh; who consider the doctrine of rewards and punishments beyond the grave to be an absurdity, and unhesitatingly become scoundrels as often as the opportunity arises, provided the authorities cannot reach them with their laws. To the third class, I finally count those who teach the people and have taken an <term>official oath</term> <note>original: "Amtseid"; specifically referring to the oaths of orthodoxy taken by clergy or professors to uphold specific church doctrines</note> that they will always represent the opinions of the <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>The influential early Christian theologians whose writings established traditional orthodoxy</note> as divine truths, even if they should doubt one or the other of them.

My book cannot harm readers of the first class; rather, it must be of use to them, because they will grant me that I have sought the truth. They find in the traditional systems of <term>popular religions</term> <note>original: "Volksreligionen"; referring to the established, institutionalized faiths of the common people as opposed to a "natural religion" based on reason</note> various dogmas that contradict not only reason but also each other, often directly. God is, as almost every religion teaches, most perfect in all His attributes. But to the highest perfection belongs no suffering. Nevertheless, God is said to have taken on a human body and suffered on earth for mankind, merely to satisfy His own justice. Now, however, there are infinitely many <term>globes</term> <note>original: "Erdkugeln"; the author is referencing the Enlightenment-era scientific belief in the "plurality of worlds," suggesting that other planets are inhabited by rational beings</note>, on all of which surely creatures similar to humans dwell, who without doubt are not quite perfect, not quite without <term>sin</term>, just as we are—otherwise they would have to be equal to God, that is, God Himself in the literal sense. Should God then go from one world, from one globe to another, to [save] all...

<summary>The author categorizes his audience into three groups (truth-seekers, scoffers, and dogmatic clergy) and begins arguing that traditional doctrines, such as the incarnation and atonement, are logically inconsistent with the idea of a perfect God and the existence of other inhabited planets.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, theological critique, Church Fathers, religious hypocrisy, plurality of worlds, reason vs. dogma, sin, justice of God</keywords>

to redeem all people from the punishments of hell through his suffering, through his bodily death? Thus his existence would consist in a constant humiliation, in a constant suffering and dying for his creatures, who by their nature can never be perfect, can never be equal to him? But how can God demand highest perfection from his creatures when he has not given them the capacity to become perfect? Such and many other considerations present themselves to the truth-loving thinker. Then he concludes that neither the Bible nor the <term>Apostolic Creed</term> <note>A foundational statement of Christian belief dating back to the early church, used to define orthodox faith</note> is the true Word of God, but rather a <term>human ordinance</term> <note>original: "Menschensatzung"; a term used to describe laws or traditions created by people rather than being divinely revealed</note>. Then he examines the prophetic scriptural passages interpreted as referring to the Messiah, and finds highly striking agreement between these old prophecies and those events that occurred with Jesus. Now he asks: How then did the prophets know all this, if they did not know it through divine revelation? So their writings would be the Word of God after all? Nevertheless, he can in no way reconcile the doctrines of the person and the <term>mediatorial office</term> <note>original: "Mittleramt"; the theological concept of Jesus acting as a bridge or mediator between a holy God and sinful humanity</note> of the Messiah with those concepts that one must necessarily form of God, and now he concludes: either God is angry and vengeful, like a headstrong master who demands supernatural services from his subjects that they cannot possibly perform, and afterwards tortures himself as punishment just to satisfy his <term>justice</term> <note>original: "Gerechtigkeit"; here referring to the "satisfaction theory" of atonement, where God's justice requires a penalty for sin</note>, or all our fundamental laws of thought, all our generally recognized truths, are mere phenomena, mere imaginings,

<summary>The author outlines the internal struggle of a rationalist thinker who finds biblical prophecies compelling but cannot reconcile the traditional doctrine of Christ's atonement with a logical or benevolent view of God. He presents the dilemma between believing in a "vengeful master" deity or concluding that human reason itself is an illusion.</summary>

# Preface.

VI

...delusions, mere <term>nerve spasms</term> <note>original: "Nervenkrämpfe"; in 18th-century thought, this refers to the idea that thoughts are merely mechanical or physical reactions of the body rather than products of a soul</note>, no fundamental laws of thought, no basic truths from which I can deduce new truths. How do matters stand now regarding the duration <note>referring to the immortality or persistence of the soul after death</note> of my soul? Am I perhaps myself merely a phenomenon? Thus he wavers anxiously over terrifying abysses of agonizing doubts of death and life to the left and to the right, like a reed that the wind bends now to this side, now to that, threatening to break it at any moment. Thus I too wavered in mortal fears, and nothing could protect me from the fall but God by means of my reason. At least I believe I know for certain that I now stand firm. Should I still waver, still err, I ask for kind and thorough instruction; for I seek only truth, because without it I cannot be happy.

Readers of the second class will also find in my book no poison, but rather healing medicines for their sick insights, if they read it with deliberation and with a love for the truth. **Voltaire** <note>François-Marie Arouet (1694–1778), the French Enlightenment philosopher famous for his wit and attacks on organized religion</note> has made them into mockers of old traditions and doctrines: here they find no mockery, but rather serious searching for truth; at the very least, they must not take those passages that perhaps sound harsh or mocking in a mocking sense. It is not at all necessary to mock the artificial weaving of the old prophetic and mystery-philosophy, but merely to investigate from where the material for it was taken, and how it was woven together. **Voltaire** has made them mockers of immortality:

# Preface.

VII

...made <note>This completes a phrase from the previous page, likely "at least they must not take those passages... in a mocking sense. It is not at all intended to make [them mockery]"</note>: here, however, they find, as I believe, sufficiently proven that we are indeed immortal, and are sacredly bound to far more good deeds than the laws demand, even if the Bible were not the Word of God, and some religion were a mere system of ceremonies. Unfortunately, I have learned to my sorrow that all the <term>Voltaireans</term> <note>followers of the French philosopher Voltaire (1694–1778), who were often accused by contemporary critics of moral nihilism and selfishness</note> I have met are not good people, but rather scoundrels, who spare neither friend nor confidant if they can only satisfy their belly, their lust, their greed, or their pride, and can only pass for honest people in the eyes of the common crowd. I speak expressly only of those I have come to know. Perhaps there are some to whom this observation does not apply: but I do not know of them. I have certainly also met many Christian scoundrels: but may God preserve me from mankind if all the Christians I knew were such people as all the Voltaireans who have made themselves known to me! According to their principles, they must indeed be as I have found them, and I would be very happy if some of them would allow themselves to be converted by my book.

To readers of the third class <note>likely referring to clergy or state officials who are bound by oaths to uphold established church doctrines</note>, this book can likewise bring no disadvantage. After all, they have not sworn off the use of their reason, but have merely confirmed by oath to teach nothing publicly that runs contrary to the prevailing religious opinions. My book, however, even shows them the most valid reasons why they should not [teach] against the prevailing...

->### VIII Preface.<-

...declaim doctrines, much less are they permitted to make unauthorized changes to them, even if they recognize many things as false. No single man at all can or may make changes in matters of religion: for he alone can easily err, and with his innovations do more harm than good, since they affect the whole too much, and consequently damage the whole if they are harmful. Only the wisest of whole nations taken together, not individually, can perhaps gradually effect changes to old harmful customs—which are nonetheless sacred to the people—with good success. Without so-called **revealed religion** <note>religion based on divine revelation (like the Bible) rather than just human reason</note>, the man who does not think for himself cannot survive; and his **mysterious little sayings** <note>original: "geheimnißvollen Sprüchelchen"; likely referring to the rote memorization of the catechism or dogmatic formulas</note>, which he learns by heart as a boy without understanding any of it, he cannot easily exchange in old age for others which he must learn anew and perhaps also does not understand. 

**Rational school instruction** in the lower classes, and in small towns or in villages, will therefore likely remain the best means for the cultivation of rational-thinking, good people. Thus, **teachers of the people** <note>referring to local clergy and schoolmasters responsible for the moral education of the public</note> can also read my book without hesitation. If they seek truth and use their **reason** for good, they will know how to use the truth with wisdom, like men who look to the well-being of the whole. But if they do not seek truth, if they have renounced the use of their reason, and if they consider **rational doubt** to be a sin, then my book cannot harm them in any case, because it merely

->### IX Preface.<-

...contains **rational arguments** <note>original: "vernünftige Räsonnements"</note>, which they simply reject.

Now, something more must be said regarding the various treatises that appear in this book.

It is well-known that many passages of the Bible, but especially the imagery of the **Apocalypse** <note>the Book of Revelation</note>, cloud the minds of many people and even incite crimes. **Rosenfeld** <note>Hans Peter Rosenfeld (1732–1803), a self-proclaimed messiah in Prussia who led a small sect and was eventually imprisoned</note> and his followers use the **Prophets** and the **Revelation of John** to justify their crimes. Detailed accounts of this can be found in the first volume of the *Berlin New Monthly Journal* <note>original: "berlinischen neuen Monatsschrift", a leading publication of the German Enlightenment</note>. 

The **Moravian**-reborn shepherd <note>referring to the *Herrnhuter* or Moravian Brethren, a Pietist Christian movement known for emotional devotion</note>—who did not believe he was perfectly reborn or fully faithful unless he could sacrifice his most beloved, just as **Abraham** did—prayed to God for this cruel faith until he felt faithful enough to strike his three young sons dead with an axe and offer them as a sacrifice to God as a seal of his faith. This can be read in the first volume of the *Yearbooks of Taste and Enlightenment*. That the man believed he had done right and earned **eternal salvation** <note>original: "ewige Seeligkeit"</note> thereby, he attempts to prove from the twenty-second chapter of the **First Book of Moses** <note>the Book of Genesis, specifically the story of the binding of Isaac</note>. 

Even now, when the malcontents in the **United Netherlands** wish to insult the **Hereditary Stadtholder** <note>the *Erbstadthalter*, the chief executive of the Dutch Republic, likely referring to William V during the political unrest of the 1780s</note>, they make use of biblical prophecies and call him the **Antichrist**. Whether they do so in jest or in earnest, I do not know; I only know that such writings and

->### X Preface.<-

...speeches are in all cases nonsensical, abominable, stupid, and evil. How many people has the mystery of the **Trinity** <note>the Christian doctrine of God as three persons in one being</note> already made into fools? How many have had their heads turned by the **Whore of Babylon**, the beast with seven heads, and the one with two horns? <note>These are apocalyptic images from the Book of Revelation, often used in the 18th century for political and religious polemics</note> How often have rulers, and how often have great cities, been compared to those beasts, dragons, and whores? If one searches carefully in the **asylums** <note>original: "Tollhäusern", literally 'madhouses'</note>, one will find that almost as many have lost their minds because of the Bible as because of unrequited love. Of course, it is never said: "The Bible robbed these people of their senses"; rather, it is merely said: "These people have over-studied." But if one studies only the **sciences** <note>original: "Wissenschaften", referring here to systematic, rational knowledge</note>, and not mysteries or **astrological fancies** <note>original: "Wahrsagergrillen"</note>—of which the Bible contains very many—one will never over-study, because sciences do not darken the mind, but rather enlighten it.

Should one not, therefore, be allowed to say how those ancient soothsaying-tales originated and how they attained the dignity of divine truths?

On page 78, I asked tentatively whether the four **ages of the world** <note>referring to the Golden, Silver, Bronze, and Iron ages described by ancient poets like Hesiod and Ovid</note> of the ancients might have originated not merely from the **precession of the equinoxes** <note>the slow, cyclic shift of the Earth's rotational axis that changes the positions of constellations over thousands of years</note>, but also from the four seasons? However, this conjecture is likely not to be doubted at all. The four ages of the world were created sometimes from the four seasons, and sometimes from the movement of the equinoxes through the four ancient primary constellations of the **Zodiac** <note>original: "Thierkreiß"</note>,

->## Preface.<-

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion of the zodiacal</note> circle: the Lion, the Bull, the Aquarius, and the Scorpion. It is well known from the history of **ancient astronomy** <note>original: "Sternkunde des Alterthums"</note> and from the great **Platonic Year** <note>A period of approximately 26,000 years in which the stars complete a full cycle due to the wobbling of the Earth’s axis</note> that the ancients, even in the earliest centuries we know of, were aware of the aforementioned movement <note>the precession of the equinoxes</note>. Poets who utilized the **four ages of the world** <note>a traditional Greek/Roman concept of human history declining from a Golden Age to an Iron Age</note>, and who made use of those derived from the **precession of the equinoxes** <note>the slow shift of the constellations relative to the seasons</note>, began with the golden age. They then followed it with the silver, then the bronze, and finally the iron. This was because the Lion appeared yellow or golden; the Bull—namely that white bull into which **Jupiter** <note>the Roman king of the gods, known as Zeus in Greek</note> is said to have once transformed himself, and which indeed appears beautifully white in the sky—appeared white or silver; the old Egyptian water-bucket, or **Canopus** <note>original: "Kanobus", a reference to an Egyptian deity often depicted as a jar, here associated with Aquarius</note>, which is the Aquarius, appeared bronze; and finally, the Scorpion appeared black or iron. 

Others, however, who created their four ages of the world from the four seasons, placed the silver first, then the golden, then the iron, and finally the bronze. They did this because they began counting with Spring, or the white Bull; then they named Summer, or the yellow Lion; then they proceeded to Autumn, which is the black, iron Scorpion; and they ended with Winter, or the bronze water-bucket. 

Therefore, the first rider in the **Apocalypse** <note>the Book of Revelation in the New Testament</note> can very well ride a white horse, the second a red horse, the third a black horse, and the fourth a pale horse. The phrase: "A measure of wheat for a **drachma** <note>an ancient Greek silver coin</note>, three measures of barley for a drachma, and do no harm to the oil and wine," <note>a reference to Revelation 6:6</note> thus fits perfectly with the fruit-

->## Preface.<-

I request my readers to incorporate this small correction while reading the book.

You will also not be mistaken if you derive the **Angel Gabriel**, who likewise appears often in this book, from **Mercury** <note>the Roman messenger god; the author suggests that Gabriel's role as a divine messenger in the Bible is an adaptation of Mercury's role in mythology</note>, or equate him with him.

Regarding the prophecies of the **Messiah** <note>The "Anointed One" promised in Jewish scripture; the author aims to provide a naturalistic rather than purely theological explanation for these predictions</note>, I have not been permitted to engage in philological investigations because my book is intended to be a reader's book, not a reference work for **exegetes** <note>scholars who specialize in the critical interpretation of religious texts</note>. However, if it is believed that my explanations do not correspond to the sense of the text, I am willing to write a commentary on it and clearly demonstrate that the entire language of the prophets is exactly the language of the ancient astrologers, and owes its origin solely to the observers of the heavens. Then I will clearly show that all folk religions basically flow entirely from a common source: from the ancient observations of celestial and natural events. Heaven and nature are those great books in which all nations, where culture began to awaken, have read—and still read—almost entirely the same events from the beginning. To be sure, these events appeared in one garment for one nation and in another for another. To be sure, the arrogant <term>priests<note>original: "Sacerdoten", a Latin term for priests often used here to imply a professional or elite religious class</note></term> clothed those ancient observations of heaven and nature

XIII
->### Preface. XIII<-
<meta>catchword: some</meta>

among one nation in these, and among another in those dark shrouds, so that the **profane** <note>original: *Profanen*; here meaning the uninitiated or common people who were not part of the priestly or scholarly classes</note>, who did not belong to their learned tribes, could not find the key to their secret science. Thus it also happened that eventually the shells themselves were taken for the kernels, the poetic fictions for truths, the shadow for the essence, and the figurative representations of celestial and natural events for divine revelations. They cut up the **cloak of truth** to make a new one from it, and they believed they had created new truth themselves, although it remained hidden unnoticed beneath the fragmented cloak. They turned the cloak inside out: but the truth wrapped within it was still not recognized. In general, they looked only at the cloak, and not at the truth itself. **Jews**, **Christians**, **Turks** <note>a historical term used by the author to refer to Muslims</note>, and all **pagans** basically have only one single religion, regardless of the fact that their mythologies turn out very differently, and regardless of their belief that they have very different religions. These supposed different religions have all flowed from the **great book of heaven**, from the **book of nature**. They also all unite in the events of the heavens and nature, like many spread-out threads that converge into a common knot.

Regarding the contemplation of **Jesus** and his **disciples**, there is nothing further to report, other than that I am ready to publicly recant as soon as I have been thoroughly refuted. But perhaps [some]...

...rich autumn, and to the equitable distribution of food that autumn provides in the land where the **Zodiac** <note>original: *Thierkreis*</note> was first divided into the known constellations. I ask my readers to keep this minor correction in mind while reading the book.

You will also not be mistaken if you derive the Angel **Gabriel**, who likewise appears often in this book, from **Mercury**, or equate him with him.

Regarding the prophecies of the **Messiah**, I have not permitted myself to engage in philological investigations because my book is intended to be a reader, not a reference work for **exegetes** <note>original: *Exegeten*; scholars who specialize in the critical interpretation of religious texts</note>. Should it be believed, however, that my explanations do not correspond to the sense of the text, I am willing to write a commentary on it and clearly show that the entire language of the prophets is exactly the language of the ancient **astrologers**, and owes its origin solely to the observers of the heavens. Then I will clearly show that all **folk religions** <term>Volksreligionen|religions practiced by the general populace as part of their culture and traditions, as opposed to institutionalized or "high" theology</term> fundamentally flow from a common source: from ancient observations of celestial and natural events. 

Heaven and nature are those great books in which all nations, where culture began to awaken, have read—and still read—almost entirely the same events from the very beginning. Admittedly, these events appeared in one garment for one nation, and in a different one for another. To be sure, the haughty **priests** <note>original: *Sacerdoten*; a Latin term for priests, used here to suggest an elite or exclusionary religious class</note> clothed those ancient observations of heaven and nature

->### Preface.<-

...for one nation in these, and for another in those dark shrouds, so that the **profane** <note>original: *Profanen*; those considered uninitiated or outside the circle of "learned tribes"</note>, who did not belong to their learned tribes, could not find the key to their secret science. Thus it also happened that people eventually mistook the shells for the kernels, the fictions for truths, the shadows for the essence, and the figurative representations of celestial and natural events for divine revelations. They cut up the **mantle of truth** to make a new one out of it, and they believed they had created a new truth themselves, although it remained hidden unnoticed beneath the fragmented mantle. They turned the mantle inside out: but they still did not recognize the truth wrapped within it. In general, they looked only at the mantle, and not at the truth itself.

Jews, Christians, Turks <note>original: *Türken*; in this period, the term was frequently used to refer to Muslims in general</note>, and all pagans have, at their core, only a single religion, regardless of how differently their mythologies turn out, and regardless of the fact that they believe they have very different religions. These supposedly different religions have all flowed from the **great book of heaven**, from the **book of nature**. They also all unite in celestial and natural events, like many spread-out threads that converge in a common knot.

Regarding the reflection on **Jesus** and his disciples, there is nothing further to report than that I am ready to publicly recant as soon as I have been thoroughly refuted. But perhaps...

Had I not been able to show my readers a new harbor for their **peace of soul** <note>original: *Seelenruhe*; a state of spiritual tranquility</note>, I would never have dared to show them the shallowness and silting up of the old one; for one must take nothing from the public if one cannot give them something better in its place. All religions have arisen from the contemplation of **nature**, especially the heavens; therefore, I too have quite naturally derived my religious principles from the contemplation of nature. XV
<header>->### Preface.<-</header>

[...due to a lack] of tools, they could arrive at no complete, no true knowledge of nature; and what they did know, they presented under the veil of **hieroglyphs**, or in mystical expressions <note>The author refers here to the metaphorical use of symbols in ancient "mystery" religions to hide natural truths from the uninitiated</note>. From this, mysterious religions arose; from this, religions emerged that, to all appearances, were very different from one another. At present, clarity and agreement prevail, at least in **astronomy** and **natural science**; therefore, clarity and agreement must also prevail in the religious tenets that flow from them.

I have titled my book *Astrognostic Final Judgment* <note>original: *Astrognostisches Endurtheil*</note> because <term>astrognosy</term> <note>from Greek *astron* (star) and *gnosis* (knowledge); the study of the names and visible positions of the stars</note> is the knowledge of the apparent position and the apparent course of the stars, but does not concern itself, as <term>astronomy</term> does, with mathematical measurements of the true motion, the true size, and the true distance of the stars from one another. Among the ancients, astronomy was almost nothing more than mere astrognosy. This they combined with <term>astrology</term>, or with that deceptive art of prophesying from the constellations. From these two arts—from astrognosy and astrology—they forged their prophecies and their **religious systems**.

I call the movement of the <term>equinoxes</term> <note>original: *Nachtgleichpunkte*; points where the sun crosses the celestial equator, making day and night equal</note> **precession** <note>original: *Präzession*</note> for the reason that I am primarily looking at the constellations that move away from the aforementioned points according to the order of the signs. If one looks at the points themselves, one must say <term>recession</term> or "backward movement" instead of precession, as is clear in itself.

> Great **Emperor Joseph!** <note>Joseph II (reigned 1765–1790), the Holy Roman Emperor known for his "Josephinism" reforms and his 1781 Edict of Tolerance</note> Many wish with me in secret that a better religion might soon be introduced! Do not let our sorrowful pleading reach your ears in vain!

I do not dare to say that: for then I would have to have never cast a glance at the constitution of the **German Empire**. How could I, as a single individual, even know whether such great changes would be beneficial to the whole, or whether they would instead be harmful? One must be just as concerned with retaining what is good as with accepting what is better; that is true. But what is better? Kind **Providence** will yet make everything as good as possible. May it only graciously prevent men from killing one another anymore because of their different **religious opinions**! Revere God above all, follow the **laws** of the land in which you live, obey your master, love your neighbor as yourself—so says almost every religion. Whoever does this will be, and is, unfailingly happy. # Preface.

Such and similar passages clearly permit no tolerance. They even grant the teachers of the Church magisterial rights <note>original: "obrigkeitliche Rechte," referring to the legal authority typically held by civil government or the state</note>, which surely ought not to be. Certainly, there are many good sayings in the Bible that advocate for tolerance; however, do we not regard not only the rational and good, but also the cruel ones, as the Word of God? Now, if a legislator, who believes the entire Bible is the Word of God, happens to be in a foul mood while making laws: will he not prefer to choose the cruel passages as his standard rather than the gentle ones?

Of passages that oppose good morals, or even the majesty of worldly potentates <note>original: "weltlicher Potentaten," referring to secular rulers or sovereigns</note>, and which likewise occur quite often in the Bible, I will say nothing at all. Were they found in my book, it would have to be confiscated by law. But they are in the Bible, and yet it is not confiscated. One would not do well to do so, precisely because it also contains much that is good. Thus, one should only omit the harmful parts, while retaining the good—among which I also include the history of the Jewish nation.

For the rest, I beg you, do not search for my name! Simply refute my book if it contains errors. Let one take up the weapons of scholarship <note>original: "Gelehrsamkeitswaffen," a metaphorical call for academic or theological debate rather than legal or physical force</note> against me, and I shall be at the service of every man, if God grants me the strength. *Jablonski’s Egyptian Pantheon, Court de Gébelin’s Oriental Allegories, Hyde’s On the Religion of the Ancient Persians, Zend-Avesta, Josephus’s Jewish Antiqui-*

# Preface.

...Jewish Antiquities <note>original: "tibus Iudaicis," likely referring to Flavius Josephus’s *Antiquitatibus Iudaicis*</note>, *Censorinus on the Birthday* <note>original: "Censorinus de Die Natali," a 3rd-century Latin work on human life and chronology</note>, *Montfaucon’s Antiquity Explained* <note>original: "Montfaucon l’Antiquité expliquée," a massive pictorial encyclopedia of the ancient world</note>, *Albategnius on the Science of the Stars* <note>original: "Albategnius de Scientia Stellarum," a foundational work by the Arab astronomer Al-Battani</note>, *Plutarch*, the Bible, astronomy, reason, and so forth, shall be my weapons.

Finally, I must also answer those who might ask why I have placed beneath the title of my book: A Lesson for Freemasons <note>original: "Denkzettel für Freimaurer." While *Denkzettel* can mean a memorandum, it often implies a "lesson" or "wake-up call" intended to correct someone's behavior.</note>.

Since that Schrepferian catastrophe <note>Referring to Johann Georg Schrepfer (1730–1774), a famous charlatan and Freemason who claimed to summon ghosts using magic lanterns and mirrors; his public suicide after his frauds were exposed caused a massive scandal.</note>, which tore the mask from the deceiver's face, one admittedly hears little more about the summoning of spirits among Freemasons. Whether a spirit can appear is something I can neither deny nor affirm; but that a human being can handle spirits like dancing bears, or that the art of summoning them and making them dance at will is even the secret of a society—that is simply impossible. Therefore, I will not even bother to refute such silly opinions, but would rather combat another absurdity <note>original: "Fratze," literally "grimace" or "caricature," used here to mean a distorted or nonsensical idea.</note> that many Freemasons consider to be something true.

From various mystical writings that have appeared in recent years with the permission of high and highest superiors, one must conclude that at least one sect of Freemasons seeks the beloved **Philosopher's Stone** <note>The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>, or perhaps even believes they already possess it. All Freemasons surely cannot be afflicted with this fanatical whim <note>original: "fanatischen Grille"</note>, for I know several of them who are anything but fanatics.

# Preface.

As I have heard and read, you all certainly wish to be good citizens, useful members of human society, and honest people. However, gold-makers <term>Goldmacher: A common term for alchemists who claimed to be able to transmute base metals like lead into gold.</term> are always either useless people or deceivers, whether they are actually able to produce it or not.

If they cannot make any gold, and yet still wish to be regarded as people who understand this art, then it is clear in itself that they must be called deceivers.

If, however, they can make it without great effort and without materials that are already disguised gold <note>original: "verlarvtes Gold." This refers to a common trick where alchemists hid real gold inside chemical mixtures or hollow stirring rods to "prove" their success to gullible patrons.</note>, then they provide the public with something that has no true value, but merely an imaginary one; it is not a true commodity, yet they exchange it for real goods and the true necessities of life, and they thus defraud the public with it. As soon as a great deal of gold can be produced without effort and without high costs, its value drops immediately; it becomes cheap and is no longer a convenient means for the promotion of trade. In itself, gold can be used for nothing except perhaps making something look shiny and yellow. One should simply work and support oneself like other honest people. What would become of the world if everyone made gold and no longer wanted to work? Then they might try to eat gold and clothe themselves in gold. Gold and silver must never fall below the value determined by the needs of the miners, the smelters, and the owners of the mines—the costs they incur while these metals are extracted and refined <note>The author is arguing for a "labor theory of value," suggesting that the worth of money must be tied to the actual human effort and resources required to dig it out of the earth.</note>,

<meta>catchword: or</meta>

<keywords>alchemy, gold-making, economics, labor theory of value, deception, social utility, mining</keywords>

# Preface.

...or even be minted into coin. But if they are made without any particular effort, and without cost, then their value drops lower, and thus the state is defrauded. There is no shortage of these metals in the world: consequently, one does the world no favor by producing them. No private individual is permitted to bring gold and silver into public circulation at all: for this business belongs solely to the state, or to its sovereign. But one also need not fear that the Rosicrucians and Brothers of Solomon <note>The "Brothers of Solomon" likely refers to the "Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross," an 18th-century Rosicrucian society that claimed Solomon as a foundational figure.</note>, or whatever else they may be called, actually make these metals. They merely destroy them or calcine <term>verkalchen: An alchemical term for oxidation, where a metal is heated until it turns into a powder or "calx."</term> them, and afterwards reduce them back again <note>This refers to the process of turning a metal into an oxide and then back into a metal, which an amateur might mistake for creating the metal anew.</note>, yet are often ignorant enough to believe they can truly make them in such a way—I can do that trick as well!

However, if they make gold and silver at great expense and at the risk of losing their health—as recently did *James Price* in England (see *London Chronicle* No. 4039 and the *Gotha Scholarly Journals*, No. 26. 1782) <note>James Price (1752–1783) was an English chemist and fellow of the Royal Society who claimed to have discovered a powder that could turn mercury into gold. When pressured to repeat the experiment before witnesses, he committed suicide by drinking laurel water (cyanide).</note>—then, insofar as they make these metals, they are completely useless to the world as well as to themselves, and therefore do not deserve to be listened to at all, especially since they do not make their art publicly known for the expansion of the world's sciences. Were they to make it known, then the assayers <term>Scheidekünstler: Experts in the chemical separation and purification of metals; the precursors to modern analytical chemists.</term> might perhaps go further and invent truly good chemical products through it that would bring real benefit to the world. But they do not do this: consequently, as gold-makers, they are useless and harmful members of human society.

-># Preface.<-

If these gentlemen finally possess a **universal medicine** <term>Universalmedizin: Also known as a panacea, this was a legendary substance alchemists claimed could cure all diseases and indefinitely prolong life.</term>, which I cannot believe of them—since they, like other people, escape neither illness nor death—then they should surely make the preparation of it known to the world as well, so that all people could make use of it, if they were true friends of humanity and not mere **quacks**? "Yes! one must not cast pearls before swine, nor throw the secret before dogs!" <note>The author is quoting a common defense used by alchemists, citing Matthew 7:6 to justify their secrecy.</note> I hear them answer. Fine! But then they should also keep their secret entirely to themselves, and not write books about it that *darken the minds* of their readers. What use in all the world are such books, given that no human being is supposed to understand them unless he receives supernatural **revelation** while reading them? To me, indeed, they do not darken the mind: I understand the language that prevails in them well enough, and I know that often only the very simplest chemical processes, which any **apothecary's boy** <note>The author uses this term to emphasize that the "secrets" are actually basic, entry-level chemistry known to any pharmacy apprentice.</note> can perform, lie hidden within them under a shroud of barbaric words and silly **astrological hieroglyphs**. But is every reader shrewd enough to realize this? Does not many a person have their head turned by such writings, and is not many a person drawn away from their business by them? One might as well stay home with such secrets in the future: for today, in the sciences, secrets, miracles, and supernatural revelations no longer hold weight. **Clarity in thinking and writing** is always the first duty of every author, which he must observe if he does not wish to be hissed at.

It is remarkable that these **adepts** <term>Adepts: In the context of alchemy and hermeticism, an "adept" is someone who has supposedly mastered the secrets of the "Great Work," such as the creation of the philosopher's stone.</term> also prove the high dignity and truth of their art from the Bible, especially from the first chapters of the **Pentateuch** <term>Pentateuch: The first five books of the Bible, attributed to Moses. What things can one not prove from the Bible, what things can one not learn in it! Thieves and murderers sometimes prove the lawfulness of their deeds from it! Makers of **gold and universal medicine** do so for their art as well! Perhaps, however, they will find in my book, which I herewith present to them, many passages that can serve for their better instruction. His "History of Ancient Astronomy" was a definitive Enlightenment text that sought to explain ancient myths as astronomical observations.</note> has written about it.

In general, one can also consider my book as a contribution to the history of astronomy, if one wishes. Ebenezer, the 2nd of **February** <note>original: "Hornung." This is an archaic German name for the month of February, often used in rural or traditional contexts.</note> 1783.

# Introduction

When all heads of households were still farmers and shepherds, and all farmers and shepherds were still kings, who lived socially with their children and servants among the livestock: at that time, they initially knew no measure of time other than the duration of the apparent daily revolution of the heavens, which we call a **natural day**. Every time the sun set, they placed a stone in a designated spot and afterwards counted the days that had passed by these gathered stones; for they could not properly record the number of their days because they did not yet possess writing.

However, as these stones accumulated immensely over a few generations and could no longer be easily counted, the observers of the heavens soon found themselves compelled to seek a larger measure of time <term>Zeitmaaß</term>. They found it in the periodic changing of the moonlight and now likewise placed a stone in its place every time they observed this change. We call this measure—which actually contains twenty-nine days along with several hours and minutes—a **moon** <note>original: "Mond"; used here to mean a lunar month.</note>; but those earliest inventors of it likely estimated it at only twenty-eight days, despite the fact that long after them it was extended to thirty days by the **Chaldeans** <note>The Chaldeans were an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their early advancements in astronomy and mathematics.</note>.

From the changing of the moonlight also arose the **week**, which likewise provided a convenient measure of time. If the moon is new today, then after seven days it turns half of its illuminated side toward the earth and is called the **first quarter**. After twice seven days

<summary>This introductory page describes the prehistoric origins of time-keeping, moving from the simple counting of days with stones to the observation of lunar phases.</summary>
<keywords>timekeeping, lunar cycle, Chaldeans, natural day, prehistory, calendar history</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of timekeeping from page 3, specifically completing the explanation of how lunar phases led to the seven-day week before transitioning into the discovery of the solar year.</meta>

he turns his entire illuminated side toward us, and is called the **full moon**. After three times seven days, we again see only half of his illuminated hemisphere, and call it the **last quarter**. After four times seven days, we see him once more not at all, and call it the **new moon**. Nothing, then, was more natural or more sensible than for people to turn this short period of seven days into a measure of time <term>Zeitmaaße</term>, especially since they could use it quite conveniently to designate the elapsed quarters of the moon. Consequently, the Mosaic creation story <note>The account of the world's origin found in the biblical book of Genesis.</note> played no part in the origin of the week, nor can it have any: for creation still continues uninterrupted even now, and will likely never be completed for all eternity. But of course, to realize this, one must be well-versed in modern astronomy as well as in natural history.

Following this, those ancient observers of the heavens also noticed that the sun itself seemed to travel around the sky from west to east—slowly, to be sure, but nonetheless regularly. Since they spent most of their lives under the open sky and could therefore observe the course of the stars at leisure, they gradually became aware that during each such revolution of the sun, an equal number of days, weeks, and moons <note>In this context, "moons" refers to lunar months.</note> always passed. They therefore also made this period of revolution into a measure of time; that is, they invented the **year**, and afterwards likewise marked each one with a stone. In the present day, we have certainly determined its duration precisely; but one must not seek such exactness in remote antiquity. Initially, it was estimated at only three hundred and fifty days, or twelve twenty-nine-day moons along with two leap days; but later, when thirty days were reckoned to a moon, it was given three hundred and sixty days.

# Introduction

Now large cities emerged, and among most peoples of antiquity, those well-known priestly orders arose. These orders took over the administration of all sciences as well as divine worship, passing this knowledge down as a hereditary right to their descendants. They did not easily instruct laypeople <term>Laien: non-experts or those outside the religious order</term> in these matters; instead, they turned their knowledge of nature into sublime secrets, into which they were accustomed to initiating their children with pompous ceremonies. Furthermore, the place where those stone markers <term>Marken: physical tokens, like stones, used to count time</term> of elapsed measures of time <term>Zeitmaaße</term> were kept acquired a kind of sanctity—just as the aforementioned markers themselves were held in exceedingly high esteem due to their great age. For this reason, the priests did not reduce those older markers of days, weeks, and moons into markers of years (as would have been proper to do after the invention of the year). Instead, they left them all lying together because they would otherwise have had to throw some away. And old things, once they are in a holy place, are not thrown away even by us enlightened peoples, however much they often disgrace not only common sense but also chronology <note>The author is likely making a satirical jab at 18th-century religious traditions that clung to outdated or illogical dogmas.</note>.

When later descendants of these first priests came upon the markers—which had been collected partly by their ancestors and partly by the very earliest observers of the heavens—they took them all to be markers of regular solar years. This happened because they did not know that in former times, people had not counted solely by solar years, but also by moons, and even by weeks and individual days. This is the reason why many a nation boasts of such a great age for its religious system and political constitution; it is also why Moses attributes such a great age to the so-called patriarchs <note>The "patriarchs" refer to biblical figures like Methuselah, who were said to live for hundreds of years.</note>. Regarding the many years which these latter figures are said to have lived: one clearly sees that these were only moons <note>The author proposes a "rationalist" theory common in the Enlightenment: that biblical "years" were actually lunar months, making the impossible lifespans scientifically plausible.</note>. # Introduction

...divide, and one never obtains more than approximately seventy to eighty solar years, or the usual age for those destined for a natural death.

Although more than a millennium may have passed from the beginning of human culture until the invention of the solar year, people at that time still knew no form of writing. Consequently, they had no means by which they could have recorded the quantities of elapsed moons and years in a convenient way to hand them down to posterity. Instead, they found themselves forced to count all things present in large quantities on their fingers. Since they had no more than ten of these, they always counted only up to ten; in this way, decades <term>Dekaten: groups of ten</term> were born. From just as many decades as a person usually has fingers, centuries <term>Centurien: groups of a hundred</term> were formed, and finally, from every ten centuries, millennia <term>Millenen: groups of a thousand</term> arose. Thus, humans came to the aid of their memory—which was not yet accustomed to grasping large numbers without physical images—with their fingers. And so, those periods of ten, one hundred, and one thousand moons and years, which one finds here and there in antiquity, received their existence. This method of calculating and counting, perfectly suited to human nature, spread so far that the ancestors of Romulus, and later the Romans themselves, even made a period of ten moons into their civil year. We still have awkward traces of this today in the Latin names of the last four months of our year. <note>The author refers to the months September, October, November, and December. In Latin, these names mean the seventh, eighth, ninth, and tenth months respectively, reflecting an ancient ten-month calendar that began in March.</note>

After humans had invented not only the year but also decimal calculation <term>Dezimalrechnung: the system of counting by tens</term>, they no longer needed to collect stones to designate elapsed solar revolutions <term>Sonnenrevoluzionen: the completion of the Earth's orbit around the sun; years</term> or other measures of time and pass them to posterity: for to

# Introduction

They were now able to achieve this goal in a simpler manner. When they wished to hand down their dates, or reports of remarkable events, and simultaneously indicate their duration, this was done, as is well known, partly through oral tradition and partly through pictorial representation. In the first case, they used their fingers or other groups of ten things as markers. In the second case, they likely drew decades <term>Dekaden: groups of ten</term> as a pair of hands with outstretched fingers, while centuries <term>Centurien: groups of a hundred</term> were perhaps drawn like ten claws or horns, and so on. But indeed, these fingers may often have looked merely like straight lines, while the claws or horns—which in later times were also made into symbols of strength—likely resembled nothing more than thick hooks. For in those days, people were certainly much further behind in the visual arts than in astronomy <term>Sternkunde: the study of the stars</term> and all other sciences, despite the fact that these, too, were still lying asleep in their cradle.

Finally, writing was invented: the only certain means of handing down reports of remarkable events and elapsed measures of time to posterity in a truly intelligible way. People continued to observe the course of the celestial lights, and now they wrote down the results of these observations in an orderly fashion to leave them to their descendants. These descendants later compared their own observations with those of their ancestors and newly discovered various measures of time consisting of great periods, which were used for the calculation of eclipses, the conjunctions <term>Konjunktionen: the meeting of two celestial bodies in the sky</term> of the planets, and other celestial events. Of this kind, for example, was not only that great period of the Chaldeans <note>The Chaldeans were an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous in the ancient world for their advanced knowledge of astronomy and astrology.</note> which contained six hundred solar years, and after whose passing the two great lights of the world <note>original: "Weltlichter"—a common historical term for the Sun and the Moon.</note> almost always come together again in the same meridian from which they departed at the beginning, but also the—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astronomical cycles that began on the previous page. The author is transitioning from the scientific observation of celestial patterns to how these observations led early civilizations to develop mythology, polytheism, and astrology.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

...the well-known golden number <term>goldene Zahl: the 19-year Metonic cycle used to harmonize the lunar and solar calendars</term>, after whose course the sun and moon likewise find themselves together again at almost exactly the same place in the heavens where they stood together at the beginning of it; this also bears the name of the period of **Meton**, although it was already known in Babylon and Egypt long before this philosopher. To this belong, along with many others, the orbital periods of the planets, as well as the great Platonic World Year <term>platonische Weltjahr: the cycle of the precession of the equinoxes, roughly 25,800 years</term>, which lasts nearly twenty-six thousand solar years and actually comprises the revolution of all the fixed stars around the axis of the ecliptic.

Since these observers of the heavens had not the slightest knowledge of the laws of motion nor of other principles of modern physics <term>Naturlehre: natural science or the study of the laws of nature</term>, yet nonetheless found the most admirable order in the so very different and often seemingly tangled course of the stars, and at the same time perceived that not only wind, rain, thunder, heat, and cold, but also many other natural occurrences alternated in an orderly, periodic fashion with said course: they quite naturally concluded that in all the stars and planets there dwelt most wise spirits. These spirits, they believed, partly ordered the regular course of the stars and partly spread winter and summer, autumn and spring, scarcity and abundance, crop failure and fertility, heat and frost, and so on, over the earth according to their own will. They did not stop there: for the inclination toward the extraordinary and the miraculous is innate in all human beings. Rather, they now also believed that the political constitutions of entire nations, as well as the fates of individual people themselves, rested in the hands of these gods, and alternated in an orderly way just like their periodic orbital times. In this way, from the study of the heavens, there soon arose not only polytheism <term>Vielgötterei: the worship of many gods</term>, but also the arts of astrology and divination, the latter of which [was] by far

...which was the most important of the theological sciences at that time, and was subsequently kept very secret by the priests <note>original: "Sacerdoten," a Latin-derived term for those who perform sacred rites</note>, because they intended to derive great advantage from it, and indeed really did so, as I will immediately show.

The first priests and prophets prophesied, to be sure, about nothing more than upcoming lunar and solar eclipses, the times for sowing and harvesting, the rising and falling of the Nile, the weather, and other such matters which have their basis in the changing position of the sun—things which we now likewise predict for the public every year in our calendars. However, through this they filled the ignorant populace with great wonder, because the people did not realize that for this art only common human understanding <term>gemeiner Menschenverstand: here referring to basic empirical observation and logical deduction</term> was necessary. Instead, they held their celestial and weather prophets to be, if not gods, then at least very holy men who stood in secret alliance with the gods and consulted with them in the most sacred chambers of the temples regarding the future fate of the world.

Certainly, this delusion was bound to provide the priests with great advantage, not only increasing their prestige but also bringing them gold and other wealth. Therefore, they by no means let it pass by unused but sought to strengthen the people in this belief. They extended their art—which until then, to use our phrasing, had consisted merely of calendar-making—to political and moral changes as well. For this purpose, they invented not only all kinds of dark and ambiguous phrases, of the sort that all oracular sayings are, but also turned those time periods, which their ancestors had discovered through diligent observation of the heavens, into prophetic numbers. These, in their opinion, contained very important secrets and [prophesied] now all sorts of plagues, now...

...welfare, over this or that king, over this or that land, or even over the whole world, they were supposed to bring. In this way, they gradually turned astrology into a system, which found all the more admiration and was regarded as all the more holy, the greater the confusion, the more impenetrable the darkness, and the more artificial the ambiguities that prevailed within it.

Some of those who forged this system likely knew that it had no firm foundation; otherwise, they could have said everything clearly and would not have had to wrap their prophecies <note>original: "Weissagungen"</note> in confused images or double-tongued speech. But many were probably not intentional frauds, but rather merely weak-minded; and one can see how they could seriously present their astrological dreams as actual realities. The older sky-observers <term>Himmelsbeobachter: early astronomers who recorded planetary movements</term> had, in their orally transmitted accounts, merely used imagery to convey that after a certain number of moons and years—or after so many decades and centuries—these or those stars would complete their labors <note>original: "Arbeiten," referring here to their orbital cycles or revolutions</note>, and begin anew once more. But the true sense of these traditions and images had, by then, largely faded away among the priests. These men imagined nothing but gods within the stars, and nothing but the management of earthly events within their "labors." From these principles it followed, of course, that the gods, by completing their labors, brought entire states to ruin through great plagues, but through the new beginning of those cycles, they drew those states forth from nothingness once again.

This religious reasoning <term>Räsonnemens: from the French word for a line of logic or argument</term>, and those great advantages that accrued to the priests through astrology, tremendously fueled their obsession with prophetic numbers, and they

<meta>This page continues the argument that ancient astrological systems were built on arbitrary observations, specifically how priests converted natural disasters into "prophetic numbers" to predict recurring cycles of misery.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Introduction 11</header>

they created a vast quantity of these based on the most absurd provocations. If, for example, it did not rain for several moons and a famine followed, they immediately turned the number of these moons or days into a prophetic number <term>Prophetenzahl: a specific numerical value believed to hold mystical or divinatory significance</term> which inherently contained hunger and misery. When they multiplied this number by the orbital period of one planet or another, it yielded a second period, after which the same distress was expected to return to the world every time. Thus, every tragic event gave rise to seven different periods, because they counted seven planets and assigned a specific nature to each one. The happiness and pleasure of entire nations do not stand out as much as tribulation and general national distress; therefore, they rarely created periods with a positive meaning. This makes it sufficiently clear why most of these periods are of a horrific nature. In the same way, they created new prophetic numbers whenever it rained too much for several moons, or when the lands where they resided were laid waste by pestilence, war, and locusts. In short, the obscurities and confusions that crept into this science due to the many absurd additions became incredibly vast.

All the so-called mystical numbers in the Apocalypse <note>original: "Apokalypse," referring here to the biblical Book of Revelation</note> and in the writings of the Prophets are of this same kind. They are nothing but astrological periods that owe their origin partly to certain natural events and partly merely to the fertile imagination of the Chaldeans <term>Chaldäer: an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their early developments in astronomy and astrology</term> and the Egyptians. These numbers were held to be matters of great importance regarding their nation by Jewish scholars—who were likewise infected with that "prophecy-plague" <note>original: "Weissagungseuche," a derogatory term used by the author to describe a mania for divination</note>—as shall become clearer in the following sections when I examine the nature of these numbers more closely.

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

The same fate that the original measurements of time of the ancients had to suffer through these astronomical mistreatments by later <term>priests <note>original: "Sacerdoten"</note>: a term used here to describe a formal class of ritual leaders who corrupted earlier natural observations</term> also befell those symbols through which the earliest sky-observers, for lack of writing, strove to transmit their reports of occurring celestial and natural phenomena to posterity — Yet I must explain myself more clearly regarding this sentence, and thus return once again to the highest antiquity, in order to investigate how those strange prophetic images might actually have originated.

Above, we have seen that first the sun, and afterwards the moon, drew the eyes of men before all other celestial bodies. Since the sun, with its bright radiance, darkened every star that stood above the horizon at the same time as it, and consequently did not allow anything else to be observed in the sky in its presence other than its daily course from east to west, or perhaps its rising and falling from south to north and from north toward south; the moon, on the other hand, at least left the stars of the first three magnitudes <note>a measurement of stellar brightness; stars of the "first magnitude" are the brightest in the sky</note> visible every night, especially when it appeared only half-full or crescent-shaped. Thus, nothing was more natural than that the observers of its course and its phase-change would particularly notice those stars where it underwent a <term>heliacal <note>original: "heliakisch"</note>: an astronomical term for when a celestial body disappears into the glare of the sun or emerges from it</term> setting every time—that is, where it hid itself under the sun's rays on the morning horizon. For the ancients always considered this <term>heliacal</term> setting to be the end of the old moon, just as they considered its <term>heliacal</term> rising, or its emergence from the sun's rays on the evening horizon, to be the beginning of the new one. Every time it set <term>heliacally</term>, they would, as mentioned, place a stone in a sacred place chosen for that purpose:

and <note>catchword: und</note>

<header>Introduction</header>

...and from this it is sufficiently clear why they carefully observed this event, and why the constellation in which the moon appeared for the last time each cycle drew their entire attention.

Now, however, the moon does not always set <term>heliacally <note>from the Greek 'heliakos,' meaning 'of the sun.' A heliacal setting occurs when a celestial body—like the moon or a star—disappears into the brightness of the morning or evening twilight because of its proximity to the sun.</note></term> near the exact same constellation: for each time, it moves forward by about a twelfth part of the <term>zodiac <note>original: "Thierkreis," literally 'animal circle'; the belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs.</note></term> toward the east. For this reason, the ancients immediately organized the stars they found in these twelve locations in the sky into twelve distinct groups. For the sake of convenience in thinking and speaking, they imagined each of these under an image that had some relation to the natural events occurring at the end of each lunar month in their land and among their livestock.

Thus, the star group at which the moon set heliacally while the sheep were lambing was designated with a lamb—which, however, was later transformed into a **Ram** <note>the zodiac sign Aries</note>. After four weeks, the moon set heliacally once again, and around this time most cows calved: therefore, the star group where it now disappeared was shaped like a calf, or also like a young **Bull** <note>the zodiac sign Taurus</note>. Again, after approximately four weeks, the goats gave birth: consequently, the constellation where the moon now appeared for the last time in the morning twilight was given the shape of two young goats, which other peoples soon thereafter transformed into the two twin brothers, **Castor** and **Pollux** <note>the zodiac sign Gemini; named after the famous twins of Greek mythology</note>. The following moon faded in the morning before the rising of the sun, just as the sun had reached the northern <term>tropic <note>original: "Wendekreiß," literally 'turning circle'; specifically the Tropic of Cancer, the northernmost point where the sun appears to 'turn' and head south again.</note></term> and now began to sink back down toward the south: for this reason, the stars that now appeared next to the sun alongside the fading moon were represented under the image of a **Crab** <note>the zodiac sign Cancer</note>, because this animal is also accustomed to walking backward. After this came the harvest: therefore, the constellation in which the moon now set <term>heliacally <note>original: "heliakisch." Referring to a celestial body rising or setting at the same time as the sun, making it briefly visible in the twilight.</note></term> had to represent a young **reaper woman** <note>the zodiac sign Virgo</note>, who held a sheaf of grain in one hand. Afterward, the moon faded shortly before sunrise when dangerous autumn fevers were rampant: consequently, the group of stars in which it lost its light this time had to represent a poisonous **Scorpion** <note>the zodiac sign Scorpio</note>. 

When the moon once again made itself invisible, they found it beneficial to hunt the game <note>original: "Wildpret"</note> that had grown fat during the summer and autumn, and to use its meat for delicacies and its skins for clothing: consequently, the stars that now appeared near the waning moon had to form a **wild man** or **Archer** <note>the zodiac sign Sagittarius</note>. That the nation where these images were invented lived in the **northern temperate zone**, and indeed at a considerable latitude, I will mention only in passing and give no extensive proof of it, because it is already clear that winter, spring, summer, and autumn follow one another in the listed order only under this climate zone, and as is well known, observe a completely different order in every other terrestrial belt. Around the **summer solstice**, the moon’s light faded in **Cancer** <note>the Crab</note>: consequently, at the beginning of summer, the sun stood in **Leo** <note>the Lion</note>. Since, furthermore, the solstices and equinoxes in the temperate zones—as well as in the cold zones—brought about the most remarkable changes in nature on earth then just as they do now, the oldest observers of the heavens made those four images in which the sun stood at the beginning of the four seasons, namely **Taurus**, **Leo**, **Scorpio**, and **Aquarius**, into the most distinguished constellations of the **zodiac** <note>original: "Thierkreis"</note>. Above all others, the one we call the **Celestial Wain** <note>original: "Himmelswagen"; commonly known today as the Big Dipper or the Great Bear (Ursa Major)</note> first drew the eyes of observers: for, because of the seven bright stars that shine within it, it is the most magnificent group of the northern sky, and at the latitude where those very same ancient astronomers lived, it never sets, but instead turns there incessantly above the horizon around the Pole. They therefore depicted this constellation like a threshing ox, which its driver, **Bootes** <note>The Herdsman</note>, drove in a circle around the threshing floor. To simultaneously indicate the constant duration of this circular path, they added a circle or a snake biting its own tail <note>This is the Ouroboros, an ancient symbol of eternity and the cyclical nature of the universe</note>, which even then was the image of a long life, indeed even of eternity. From this snake they later formed the **Dragon** <note>the constellation Draco</note>, which, taken in another sense—as I shall show in the future—gave rise to many kinds of prophetic tales among later peoples, and which even now winds itself through the heavens in our star catalogs between the Great and Little Bear.

Other remarkable constellations that shone brightly in the eyes of the stargazers were **Orion** and the **Greater Dog** <note>Canis Major, containing the star Sirius</note>. Through continued observations, mankind <note>OCR error in original: "Menferner" likely intended as "Menschen ferner" (men further)</note> further learned of the great influence of the light of the **Sun** on the generation and preservation of animals and plants. From the Sun, they drew conclusions about the **Moon**, and believed that it likewise played a large part in the work of generation and preservation, because it appeared to them to be the most eminent celestial body after the Sun. Therefore, they presumably depicted these two celestial lights as man and wife, who were engaged in the very act of procreation, and from whom the generated life of animals and plants emerged in a third form. Now, according to the concepts of that time, the Sun and Moon were the two most eminent gods, and were already represented as two persons; consequently, one felt compelled to give them the five remaining **planets** as companions in human form, and to provide them with attributes that had some relation <note>The text breaks mid-word at "Bezie-"; the catchword "hung" indicates the word is "Beziehung" (relation)</note> to their various orbital periods or their various brilliance...

# Introduction

...relationship <note>original: "hung", likely from "Zusammenhang" (connection/relationship)</note> they had. Thus the images of the seven planets arose, which afterwards had to lend their names to the seven days of the week, and so forth.

From these few examples of the works of the earliest **star-gazers** <note>original: "Sternseher"; an early term for astronomers</note>, it follows that the other images of the stars could have arisen, and certainly did arise, from similar circumstances.

Once the star groups and planets, as well as atmospheric phenomena <note>original: "Lufterscheinungen"</note> and various other things occurring in nature, had been rendered into images in the manner described, they were simultaneously used as a convenient means of passing down reports of remarkable natural events to posterity. For example, if one wanted to describe the **conjunction** <note>original: "Zusammenkunft"; literally a "gathering" or "meeting" of planets</note> of several planets, their images were drawn in their proper connection, and to this was added the animal or constellation <note>original: "Gestirn"</note> in which they appeared together within the **zodiac** <note>original: "Thierkreis"</note>. If one wished to describe the **heliacal rising** <term>heliacal rising: the first time a star or planet becomes visible in the eastern sky just before sunrise</term> of a planet, it was placed upon the animal or constellation in which it appeared before the sun's rays near the horizon, as if on a mountain; and beneath it, the sea was drawn, from which it supposedly climbed upward—that is, rose above the horizon. In the same way, its **heliacal setting** <term>heliacal setting: the last time a celestial body is visible in the western sky just after sunset before being lost in the sun's glare</term> was painted like a man whom the sun killed or cast down from heaven. In this manner, all natural events were treated. It is clear from what has been said previously that the **decades** and **centuries** of elapsed moons or years were added to these images, and that they were consequently adorned with hands, talons, and claws or horns <note>The author suggests that physical attributes on mythological figures were actually symbolic codes for spans of time</note>.

Since the earliest humans, as mentioned, had not the slightest knowledge either of the laws of motion or of other principles of modern **natural science** <note>original: "Naturlehre"; the study of nature or physics</note>,

# Introduction

...observed, and consequently, upon looking at the heavens, could very easily fall upon the idea that not only the planets but also the other stars were living beings who, by their own power, wandered regularly across the sky without bumping into anything, and by their own goodness blessed the world with their fertilizing breath—in short, that these wonderful lights were great gods. Thus, soon after their creation, the aforementioned images attained a very great dignity because, in the eyes of the learned as well as the ignorant masses, they now represented nothing less than gods. The priests therefore preserved them in the most sacred manner within the temples, and assigned to them a value just as high, if not higher, than our clergy assign to the writings of the prophets and apostles. People continued to make new observations and likewise represent them through images. And from this it is easy to infer that their variety and quantity must have gradually become exceedingly large, and their deciphering uncommonly difficult for later priests.

When writing <note>original: "die Schrift"; referring here to the invention of alphabetic or phonetic scripts compared to older pictorial systems</note> was finally invented and generally introduced, the true meaning of these images—which, as mentioned, contained nothing but reports of astronomical, economic, and political events—fell entirely into oblivion. No one boasted of understanding them except for the **priests** <note>original: "Sacerdoten"; a term derived from Latin emphasizing their ritual status</note>, who had indeed inherited the art of explaining them from the ancient **star-gazers** <note>original: "Sternsehern"; early astronomers</note>, their ancestors, through **tradition** <note>original: "Tradizion"</note>, but had also largely forgotten it again. According to the nature of their **national taste** <note>original: "Nazionalgeschmacke"; the cultural preferences or "style" of a specific people</note>, they sometimes attributed a prophetic meaning to them, or found in them nothing but the history of great men who had immediately become gods. From this, one can easily infer why the same images in different countries and among different nations—into whose hands they fell partly through war, partly through...

...migrations, may have arrived, they gave rise to quite different concepts. This explains why the mythology of the Greeks is structured so differently from that of the Egyptians, Chaldeans, Phoenicians, Hebrews, Chinese, Brahmins, Jews, and Christians—examples shall place this proposition in a clearer light.

The oldest observers compared the sun and moon to a husband and wife, and painted a boy alongside them to represent living and growing nature which, according to the concepts of that time, was fathered and born by the sun and the moon. When the true meaning of these images had faded, the Egyptians turned the sun into an actual god and king, **Osiris**, while the moon became a physical goddess and queen, **Isis**, and the fruits of the field became a little god and boy, **Horus**. Among the Greeks, **Osiris** was transformed into **Zeus** <note>original: "Jevs"; a variant of Jove or Zeus</note>, **Isis** into **Aphrodite**, and **Horus** into **Cupid**. **Moses**, however—who, as is well known, was raised at the court of the **Pharaoh**, instructed in the sciences, and perhaps even initiated into the mysteries of **Isis**—had better concepts of God than the common crowd of Egyptian priests. He strictly forbade his **Israelites** from worshiping images and said that God was a spirit and a single God, who would be greatly angered if they tried to make an image of him. Nevertheless, they had not only become acquainted with such images as deities in **Egypt** and found pleasure in them, but they undoubtedly became even more familiar with them later during their captivity in **Babylon**. Since, by virtue of their laws, they were not permitted to look upon foreign gods, much less become acquainted with them, they transformed the three supreme deities of the Egyptians into images of the Mosaic
