history of creation, by regarding the **Sun** or the **Man** as the **Spirit of God** who hovered <note>original: "webete," a reference to the "brooding" or "hovering" of the Spirit in Genesis 1:2</note> over the **virgin waters**, the **Moon**, on the other hand—which might be depicted as a young **girl**—as the **virgin water** itself, and finally the begotten **Boy**, or vegetative nature, as the **Firstborn** of creation, the lovely **Son of eternal Omnipotence**, that is, as the **Light**. Since this **Light**, according to their poetic inventions, sprang forth at the first divine **command**: "**Let there be light!**", they subsequently called it simply the **Word**, or in Greek the **Logos**, and turned it into a third generative being. Greece's mystical philosophers, who occupied themselves with explanations of the origin of natural things and had perhaps heard of these Jewish fables, immediately transformed these three things into three **principles of nature**: into the **World Spirit** (or **Air**), into pure **Water**, and into pure **Earth**—just as later the **alchemists** rechristened the **World Spirit** as **Sulphur**, the **Water** as **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercur"</note>, and the pure **Earth** as **Salt**. In short, **Egyptians**, **Chaldeans**, **Jews**, **Greeks**, and **Christens** carved and painted away at the aforementioned images of the two great **celestial lights** <note>literally "world-lights," referring to the sun and moon</note> and their supposed offspring for so long until finally—prompted by various passages in the **New Testament**, and especially the verses:

# Introduction

Indisputably, many of the ancestors <note>original: "Stammvåter," referring to the patriarchs or earliest progenitors of a people</note> of the current human race lived under a very mild climate in Asia and worshipped only one single God. However, this worship of a single supreme being was not yet the result of philosophical reasoning, much less a supernatural divine revelation: it was merely a natural consequence of the beneficial effects of the great star of the day.

The most ancient humans, to be sure, also observed the stars of the night with great admiration; but none of these appeared to them so majestic, so brilliant, so magnificent, so large as this mighty **King of Light**. None spread so much gentle warmth, so much active life, so much joyful growth over the world as he did; indeed, the faint lights of the night even seemed to altogether crawl away <note>original: "verkriechen"</note> out of humble reverence whenever the aforementioned great King of Heaven let his arrival be announced to the astonished children of men through the purple radiance of his handmaiden, **Aurora** <note>The Roman personification of the dawn</note>.

Furthermore, it could not escape the notice of these ancestors that light and warmth came directly from the **Sun** and, in an invisible and incomprehensible way, as it were, animated not only all plants but also all animals. Therefore, their common sense <note>original: "gesunder Menschenverstand," a hallmark of Enlightenment-era thought referring to uncorrupted natural reason</note>—which had not yet been led astray by old prejudices nor given a specific direction through religious instruction—necessarily told them that this great, beneficial, immortal ball of light was the single supreme being, the only sovereign ruler of both the earth and the heavens. Since the sun thus blessed people daily and yearly with new favors and sustenance, but at times also caused drought and crop failure, they concluded that this god was, in essence, highly benevolent, yet sometimes also angry or fierce. They believed he cast blessings and curses over the world at his whim, like a headstrong man who found himself now in a good, now in a bad mood. A desire for goods and a loathing of scarcity and hunger therefore drove the minds of men to think of means that might keep this constant creator of their food always in a good mood, so that he might grant them good wine, sufficient grain, and plenty of livestock alongside lush pasture. Nothing, then, was more natural than the thought of diligently inviting this god as a guest and setting before him the best wine, the best grain, the fattest cattle, and even their most beloved children <note>The author is likely referring to historical accounts of ancient "pagan" sun-worshiping cultures, such as the Phoenicians or Carthaginians, who practiced human sacrifice.</note>. But the only problem was that he ate none of it. 

Yet they soon knew how to help themselves in this: they believed his mouth was simply raised too high above the ground for him to accept food from them. From this they concluded that the cause of these effects was a distinct invisible being, which did not depend on this ball of light, but rather produced and nourished all things on its own—In this way, they arrived quite naturally at the concept of the single, invisible, omnipresent divine being.

**Nomads** and **shepherds**, who moved about in family groups with their herds and thus received their entire well-being or woe from the various effects of heat—that is, from the weather—certainly remained faithful to this simple **religion** of their fathers: but in cities, where people concerned themselves with neither **livestock breeding** nor **agriculture**, and where food was available in abundance, they were soon no longer satisfied with such a simple, invisible God whom the peasants and shepherds worshipped. # Introduction

Here also lived those families whose ancestors had made the first astronomical, chronological, meteorological, and chemical observations, and had left them the records of these in images, which they initially used merely for their own instruction, even adding new ones themselves. These obliging, learned people—these sole **children of light** <note>A term often used in Enlightenment and Masonic circles to refer to those who possess secret or intellectual knowledge.</note>, who were then wiser than the "children of this world" or the unlearned—now set themselves up as priests. They transformed not only the planets but also the animals that were already placed in the heavens <note>A reference to the constellations of the Zodiac.</note> into gods, which they then willingly handed over to the people. But their images and **hieroglyphs** themselves, by which they well knew that only celestial and natural events were to be understood, they locked away in subterranean caves and in the innermost, most holy places of the temples, so that clever commoners <note>original: "Profane"—referring to the uninitiated or those outside the priestly caste.</note> might not see them and discover their deceptions—In this way, the **Mysteries of Antiquity** arose.

The many gods seem to have arisen gradually in the following order. Initially, as mentioned, one knew only a single God, who had been abstracted from the sun. Afterward, however, the learned philosophized about the origin of things and adopted the procreation of humans as a principle; that is, they based their doctrine of the **creation** on the concept of the three beings of procreation: **Father, Mother, and Child**. Thus the three divine beings arose, who were unquestionably known to the scholars of all nations of antiquity, only under different names. ...the Father as the Lord of Light, the Mother as the Ruler of Night or Darkness, and finally the Son as the World, which, according to their teachings, was procreated from Light and Darkness, as is known from the **Zend-Avesta** <note>The primary collection of sacred texts of Zoroastrianism, the ancient Persian religion founded by the prophet Zoroaster.</note>. Yet, for the most part, the scholars kept these invisible deities to themselves. For the common people, however, who showed a great desire for tangible images, they gradually canonized the images of the twelve signs of the **Zodiac** <note>original: "Thierkreis"</note>, the images of the twenty-four animals of the remaining heavens, the images of the planets, the symbols of the four elements, and a multitude of other natural objects.

The Egyptians seem to have been the first to add the five remaining planets and the four elements to their Trinity—consisting of **Osiris**, **Isis**, and fecund nature <note>Likely referring to Horus or the physical world as the "Son" in the Egyptian triad.</note>—which, when taken together, constituted the well-known twelve supreme gods of that nation. The Chaldeans, Persians, and East Indians, on the other hand, accepted the five planets into the rank of gods much later, presumably because of their seemingly erratic orbits. Instead, they first placed the four constellations of the solstices and equinoxes—the **Lion**, **Scorpion**, **Water-Bearer**, and **Bull** <note>Leo, Scorpio, Aquarius, and Taurus. These are the "Fixed Signs" of the Zodiac, which in antiquity roughly aligned with the four seasons.</note>—alongside the three divine beings of procreation.

Since the priests had made these gods themselves and had inevitably sold them dearly to the ignorant people as mere puppets, they certainly knew at the beginning that there was not much to them. # Introduction

...original, quite rational doctrine of divine unity, as well as all symbols and images of nature’s effects, extremely secret from all people who did not belong to their learned families. From this, one can sufficiently conclude that the great secret of these priestly castes was originally nothing more than *Deism* <note>The philosophical belief in a single Creator who does not intervene in the world, contrasting with the many-god systems taught to the public.</note>.

The art of calendar-making, geometry, physics, chemistry, arithmetic, and other such arts—which they had inherited from their ancestors under the shroud of imagery and studied in secret, yet practiced in public—earned them an exceedingly high reputation among the uninitiated <note>original: "Profanen"; those outside the inner circle of the priesthood.</note>. They were regarded by them as humans of a higher sort, as the chosen ones of the gods, as companions of the stars, as saints, and even as demigods, and were immensely feared. Because of their calendar-making, they were held to be great prophets to whom the gods revealed all future events of the world within the mysteries. The physical and mathematical sleight-of-hand <note>original: "Taschenspiele"; literally "pocket-plays" or magic tricks used to demonstrate power.</note> with which they dazzled the people were seen as supernatural sorceries. Perhaps they also prepared medicines that produced healing effects in many illnesses; and for this reason, they were hailed by the astonished rabble as gods who could grant health and long life to mortals at will. Likely they were finally also able to transform copper into brass <note>original: "Mössing"</note>, metals into lime <note>original: "Kalch"; referring to calcination, an early chemical process of heating ores.</note>, and afterwards transform this lime back into metal. Instantly the ignorant crowd cried out: "These people are creators! They can make gold from copper, and metal from earth!"

Enticed by this delusion of the people—which was indeed simple-minded, but very advantageous for the priests—these men now devoted themselves entirely to the practice of such arts, and finally made them the actual secret—

...knowledge of their order. By teaching these things to one another merely mechanically from the old images, without understanding the true natural causes of their sleight-of-hand <note>original: "Gaukelspiele"; referring to deceptive tricks or illusions used to maintain power.</note> themselves, they completely forgot the original doctrine of the one God—the first true secret of their society—and even the true meaning of their secret books. They then came to regard themselves exclusively as the chosen and the holy, whom the gods taught to produce supernatural effects in the world by means of those secret hieroglyphs and images. 

What is more! Even today, there are gloomy minds <note>original: "düstere Köpfe"; likely a critique of the author's contemporaries in the 18th-century Enlightenment who remained obsessed with the occult.</note> who fancy themselves wise, and who not only believe these ancient experimental physicists and calendar-makers were the actual possessors of the beloved **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone: A legendary alchemical substance said to be capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</term> and supernatural magical arts, but also believe that there are still such adepts <note>original: "Adepten"; individuals who have attained a high level of secret or "hidden" knowledge.</note> and sorcerers in certain secret societies today who could even prove their sciences from the first chapters of the **Pentateuch** <term>Pentateuch: The first five books of the Hebrew Bible, which many occultists believed contained encoded alchemical or magical instructions.</term>!

However, the assertion that the initiates gradually forgot the first secret—namely, the doctrine of the unity of God and of the triad of creation <note>original: "Dreien der Erzeugung"; likely referring to the creative forces or a primitive trinitarian concept in ancient cosmology.</note>—seems to apply primarily to Egypt and Babylon, where one finds, as it were, the home of all conjuring tricks, all the deceptions of soothsayers, and all magical antics. In Persia and East India, this knowledge was preserved for much longer. Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet, also known as Zarathustra.</note> is said to have introduced the mysteries there and to have given his initiates **Mithra**—the pure eternal light, or the fire of the sun—as the supreme God, while subordinating the other celestial bodies to him as mere demons. and it is well known that the worship of fire has continuously survived in many places in the Orient.

<meta>This page continues the "Introduction," moving from the loss of original knowledge by ancient priests to the specific evolution of these secret doctrines as they transitioned from Egypt to Greece. It discusses how the philosophical Greeks interpreted Egyptian symbols and the specific rituals used in the mysteries of Mithras and Pythagoras.</meta>

# Introduction

From the mysteries of *Isis* were formed the mysteries of *Ceres*, which subsequently arrived in *Athens*. The greater mysteries of this kind clearly also had the knowledge of the single God—the creator of all things and the soul of the world—as their primary subject. However, in this context, *Deism* <term>Deism: The belief in a supreme being or creator based on reason rather than revelation.</term> was no longer a natural consequence of the beneficial effects of the sun, but rather a result of the philosophical reasoning of thinking Greeks, who were able to successfully decode the true meaning of the ancient Egyptian symbols representing the effects of nature. Within these mysteries, the triple divine essence of procreation was undoubtedly taught as well; this is proven partly by depictions of male and female reproductive organs, and partly by grains of corn, cakes, pomegranates, ivy, poppies, and other fruits of nature that were carried about in the mysteries of *Bacchus*, *Isis*, and *Ceres*. Regarding *Pythagoras*, who fashioned his mysteries from East Indian, Persian, *Chaldean*, *Egyptian*, *Orphic*, *Eleusinian*, and *Samothracian* sources, it is at least reliably known that he taught the Trinity and Unity of God.

Whoever wished to be initiated into the mysteries of *Mithras* <note>original: "Mithra"; refers to the Roman cult of the god Mithras, which involved complex initiation grades.</note> had to endure eighty difficult trials, as *Nonnus* <note>original: "Nonus"; likely referring to Nonnus of Panopolis, whose commentaries describe the grueling trials of the Mithraic initiates.</note> reports. That is, he had to fast <gloss>fast</gloss> <note>original: "f n"; an abbreviation for "fasten."</note> for many consecutive days, frequently swim across a wide river, run through fire, live in solitude, and so forth. Once the candidates had survived these trials, they were then baptized and marked on their foreheads with secret signs. Honey was smeared upon their tongues; such ceremonies were also observed during the admission of new members in other types of mysteries, as is known from the history of *Pythagoras*, who sought initiation in every place where such societies existed.

<meta>catchword: Upon</meta>

<summary>This page explores how Greek philosophers adapted Egyptian mysteries into their own systems, focusing on the transition from symbolic nature worship to philosophical Deism and describing the rigorous physical trials required for initiation into the cult of Mithras.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Ceres, Athens, Deism, Bacchus, Pythagoras, Mithras, initiation rituals, secret societies, ancient mysteries</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Introduction," shifting from the historical rituals of Pythagoras and Mithras to a rationalist interpretation of the Egyptian and Greek mysteries. It explains the myths of Osiris and Proserpine as allegories for astronomical and agricultural phenomena.</meta>

# Introduction

At the celebration of the mysteries of **Isis**, it was customary to dramatically represent **Osiris**, who was torn apart by a cruel dragon, **Typhon**, but whose bloody remains were then gathered again by **Isis**. **Osiris** was the sun. The dragon represented the node of the moon's orbit, still called the <term>Dragon’s Head or Dragon’s Tail: Historical astronomical terms for the ascending and descending lunar nodes, the points where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path (the ecliptic), which is where eclipses occur.</term> where, as is well known, all solar and lunar eclipses fall. **Isis** signified, as I have already said, the moon. Thus, this entire drama contained nothing more than a report of an eclipse of the sun, which the dragon—or the node of the moon's orbit—as it were, "ate"; but after the completion of the eclipse, the moon, as usual, gathered the remaining sunlight again and became visible anew. In the lesser mysteries of **Ceres**, on the other hand, the fable of the abducted **Proserpine** <note>Proserpine is the Roman name for Persephone, whose descent into the underworld was used by the ancients to explain the winter dormancy of crops.</note> was dramatized; she was anxiously mourned by her mother, **Ceres**, which basically indicated nothing more than a spoiled harvest or scorched grain. Because of this, the fields where it had grown grieved immensely out of compassion for the poor people who now had nothing to eat.

One sees from this that the images and fables of the ancient mysteries as a whole really contained nothing more than well-known celestial and natural events, as **Dionysius of Halicarnassus** and **Plutarch** <note>Dionysius (a historian) and Plutarch (a philosopher) both sought to explain the "hidden" meanings of religious rituals through history and science.</note> explicitly assert. This is also quite natural: for heaven and nature were, from the beginning, the great books in which all nations could read clearly and from which they could abstract consistent concepts. It is therefore not even necessary to claim that all similar ideas of dissimilar and far-distant nations must necessarily have come from a single ancestral people: for the similarity of such concepts exists in the heavens and in nature itself.

<summary>This page argues that ancient mystery cults were not based on supernatural revelation, but were dramatic allegories for natural phenomena like solar eclipses and crop failures. The author suggests that similar myths across different cultures arise from a shared observation of the natural world rather than a single common ancestor.</summary>

<keywords>Isis, Osiris, Typhon, Ceres, Proserpine, Dionysius of Halicarnassus, Plutarch, Mysteries, celestial events, natural phenomena, lunar orbit, Dragon's Head, Dragon's Tail, solar eclipse</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Introduction," transitioning from the astronomical interpretation of Egyptian myths to the rationalist developments in Greece and the rigorous initiation rites of the Mithraic mysteries. # Introduction

The mysteries of *Isis* were transformed into the mysteries of *Ceres*, which later arrived in *Athens*. The greater mysteries of this kind clearly had as their main subject the knowledge of the one *God*, the *Author* of all things, the *Soul of the World*: but here, *Deism* <note>Deism: The belief, based solely on reason, in a God who created the universe but abandoned it, assuming no control over life or nature.</note> was no longer a natural consequence of the beneficial effects of the *sun*, but rather a result of philosophical reasoning by thinking *Greeks*, who were able to successfully unravel the true meaning of the ancient Egyptian symbols of natural forces. In these mysteries, they undoubtedly also taught the triple divine essence of *procreation*: for this is proven partly by the images of the male and female reproductive organs, and partly by the grains of wheat, cakes, pomegranates, ivy, poppies, and other fruits of nature that were carried around in the mysteries of *Bacchus*, *Isis*, and *Ceres*. Regarding *Pythagoras*, who created his mysteries from *East Indian, Persian, Chaldean, Egyptian, Orphic, Eleusinian*, and *Samothracian* sources, it is at least reliably known that he taught the *Trinity* and *Unity of God*.

Anyone wishing to be initiated into the mysteries of *Mithra* <note>Mithra: An ancient Persian god of light and truth whose cult became popular across the Roman Empire.</note> had to endure eighty hard trials, as *Nonus* <note>Likely referring to Nonnus of Panopolis, a Greek poet whose work was often cited in early modern studies of ancient mythology.</note> reports; that is, he had to <unclear>fast</unclear> for many consecutive days, swim through a wide river many times, run through *fire*, live in solitude, and so forth. Once the candidates had survived these trials, they were then baptized and marked on their foreheads with secret signs. Honey was smeared onto their tongues: and such ceremonies were also observed in other types of mysteries during the admission of new members, as is known from the history of *Pythagoras*, who sought admission wherever such mysteries existed.

<meta>This page continues the "Introduction," moving from the trials of the Mithraic mysteries mentioned on the previous page to a rationalist, astronomical interpretation of Egyptian and Greek myths. The author argues that these ancient mysteries were symbolic representations of natural phenomena rather than purely mystical or supernatural events.</meta>

# Introduction

At the celebration of the mysteries of *Isis*, it was customary to dramatically represent *Osiris*, who was torn apart by a cruel dragon, *Typhon*, whose bloody remains were then collected again by *Isis*. *Osiris* was the sun. The dragon represented the node of the moon’s orbit, the still-so-called <term>dragon’s head or dragon’s tail</term> <note>These are historical astronomical terms for the lunar nodes—the two points where the Moon's orbit intersects the ecliptic (the sun's apparent path). Solar and lunar eclipses can only occur when the moon is near these nodes.</note>, where, as is well known, all solar and lunar eclipses fall. *Isis* signified, as I have already said, the moon. Thus, this entire drama contained nothing more than an account of a solar eclipse, which the dragon, or the node of the moon’s orbit, as it were, devoured; but after its completion, the moon, as usual, collected the remaining sunlight again and became visible anew. In the lesser mysteries of *Ceres*, on the other hand, the fable of the abducted *Proserpine* <note>In Greek mythology, Persephone; she was the daughter of Ceres (Demeter) and was abducted by Pluto (Hades) to the underworld.</note> was dramatized, who was anxiously mourned by her mother, *Ceres*. This signified nothing more than a ruined crop or burnt grain, over which the fields where it had grown grieved immensely out of compassion for the poor people who now had nothing to eat.

One sees from this that the images and fables of the ancient mysteries in their entirety really contained nothing more than well-known celestial and natural events, as <term>Dionysius of Halicarnassus</term> <note>A Greek historian and teacher of rhetoric (c. 60 BC–after 7 BC).</note> and *Plutarch* <note>A Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46 AD–119 AD) whose works often sought to explain myths through natural philosophy.</note> explicitly claim. This is also quite natural: for heaven and nature were, from the beginning, the great books in which all nations could clearly read and from which they could abstract consistent concepts. It is therefore not even necessary to claim that all similar ideas among dissimilar and widely separated nations must necessarily have originated from a single <term>ancestral nation</term> <note>original: "Stammvolke" — a common progenitor or "root people" from whom all culture supposedly spread.</note>: for the similarity of such concepts is found in the heavens and in nature itself.

<summary>The author provides a rationalist interpretation of Egyptian and Greek myths, explaining the stories of Osiris and Proserpine as allegories for astronomical eclipses and agricultural cycles. He argues that cultural similarities arise from universal human observations of nature rather than a single ancestral source.</summary>

# Introduction

From the mysteries of **Isis**, the mysteries of **Ceres** <note>Ceres is the Roman name for Demeter, the Greek goddess of agriculture and the harvest.</note> were formed, which afterwards came to **Athens**. The "Greater Mysteries" of this kind clearly had as their main subject the knowledge of the one **God**, the author of all things, the soul of the world; but here, **Deism** <note>In this context, deism refers to the philosophical belief in a single creator based on reason rather than revelation.</note> was no longer merely a natural consequence of the beneficial effects of the sun, but rather a result of the philosophical reasoning of thinking Greeks, who were fortunate enough to unravel the true meaning of the ancient Egyptian symbols of natural effects. In these mysteries, they also unfailingly taught the triple divine essence of procreation: for this is proven partly by the images of male and female reproductive organs, and partly by the grains of wheat, the cakes, the pomegranates, the ivy, the poppy, and other fruits of nature that were carried about in the mysteries of **Bacchus**, **Isis**, and **Ceres**. Regarding **Pythagoras**, who created his own mysteries from the East Indian, Persian, Chaldean, Egyptian, Orphic, Eleusinian, and Samothracian traditions, we know at least with certainty that he taught the Trinity and Unity of God.

Anyone who wished to be initiated into the mysteries of **Mithra** <note>Mithras was a Persian deity whose mystery cult became highly popular among Roman soldiers.</note> had to, as **Nonus** <note>Likely referring to the 5th-century poet Nonnus of Panopolis, or more specifically the "Scholia" on Gregory Nazianzen attributed to a "Nonnus," which describes these trials.</note> reports, endure eighty harsh trials; that is, he had to <unclear>fast</unclear> for many days in a row, frequently swim through a wide river, run <unclear>through</unclear> fire, live in solitude, and so forth. However, after the candidates had survived these trials, they were baptized and marked on their foreheads with secret signs. Honey was smeared upon their tongues: and such ceremonies were also observed in other types of mysteries during the admission of new members, as is known from the history of **Pythagoras**, who sought admission everywhere such mysteries existed.

<meta>This page continues the "Introduction," moving from the previous discussion on Pythagoras and the Mithraic mysteries to a naturalistic and astronomical interpretation of the myths of Isis, Osiris, and Ceres. # Introduction

At the celebration of the mysteries of **Isis**, it was customary to dramatically represent **Osiris**, who was torn apart by a cruel dragon, **Typhon**, whose bloody remains were then gathered again by **Isis**. **Osiris** was the sun. The dragon represented the node of the moon's orbit—still known today as the <term>Dragon’s Head or Dragon’s Tail</term> <note>These are traditional astronomical terms for the ascending and descending lunar nodes; they are the two points where the Moon's orbit intersects the Ecliptic (the Sun's apparent path).</note>—where, as is well known, all solar and lunar eclipses occur. **Isis** signified, as I have already stated, the moon. Thus, this entire drama contained nothing more than a report of a solar eclipse, which the dragon (or the node of the moon's orbit) "devoured," so to speak; but after its completion, the moon, as usual, gathered the remaining sunlight once more and became visible anew. In the "Lesser Mysteries" of **Ceres** <note>Ceres is the Roman name for the Greek goddess Demeter; her "Lesser Mysteries" were held annually at Agrae as a preparation for the "Greater Mysteries" at Eleusis.</note>, on the other hand, they dramatized the fable of the abducted **Proserpina** <note>Also known as Persephone; her abduction to the underworld by Pluto (Hades) caused her mother's grief, resulting in winter.</note>, who was anxiously mourned by her mother, **Ceres**; in essence, this indicated nothing more than a ruined harvest or scorched grain, over which the fields where it had grown grieved immensely out of compassion for the poor people who now had nothing to eat.

One sees from this that the images and fables of the ancient mysteries, taken as a whole, really contained nothing more than well-known celestial and natural occurrences, as **Dionysius of Halicarnassus** and **Plutarch** <note>Dionysius was a 1st-century BC historian; Plutarch was a 1st-century AD philosopher. Both were influential in interpreting mythology through historical and natural lenses.</note> explicitly assert. This is also quite natural: for from the beginning, the heavens and nature were the great books in which all nations could clearly read and from which they could abstract consistent concepts. Therefore, it is not even necessary to claim that all similar ideas found in dissimilar and far-distant nations must necessarily have originated from a single ancestral people; for the similarity of such concepts exists in the heavens and in nature itself

# Introduction

...were written so clearly that they must have been recognized very easily, at least throughout the entire northern temperate zone.

It is further to be noted that the subterranean caves in which the ancients celebrated their **mysteries** were decorated with the twelve constellations of the **zodiac**, the symbols of the seven planets, the characters of the four elements, and many other such images. From this, it again seems clearly evident that their primary intent was to study nature in order to more closely understand its fundamental essence—that is, God, or the <term>universal world-soul</term> <note>Known in Latin as the *Anima Mundi*, this is the idea that a spiritual essence permeates all nature, connecting the divine to the physical world.</note>—as **Clement of Alexandria** <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who often wrote about the relationship between Greek philosophy and religion.</note> also implies.

Now we wish to return to our text concerning the origin of these fables and images, which we left off above during the discussion of the **Christian Trinity**, and for now acquaint ourselves with just one more example of this.

The most magnificent star after the sun and moon was **Venus**, which, because of its extraordinary clarity and size, very quickly drew the attention of the earliest stargazers. When they wanted to tell posterity that it had once risen high above the horizon in its most beautiful radiance before sunrise, and had thus been the **Morning Star**, they painted the heavens and placed a man within them whose beauty and stature almost equaled the image of the sun, to indicate that it had shone in the sky almost like a small sun. But soon thereafter, the sun’s rays obscured it and brought an end to its great glory; that is, it underwent a <term>heliacal setting</term> <note>A heliacal setting occurs when a star or planet "disappears" because it becomes lost in the glare of the sun's light as it approaches conjunction.</note> along with its servants, the stars near which it stood. Thus, they painted the previous man again, but in such a way that

According to Jewish traditions, this fallen angel was named **Sammael** <note>In Jewish lore, Sammael is an archangel often associated with the figure of the accuser or the angel of death.</note>. Regarding the **old dragon**, who is said to seduce the entire world and who is also considered an evil spirit in the Christian church, I shall have the opportunity to speak in the book itself.

In a similar manner, all the images of those most ancient records—which contain absolutely nothing but astrological conjectures or astronomical, economic, meteorological, and perhaps political reports, along with their associated lunar and annual figures—have been viewed not only from entirely different perspectives but also frequently applied to quite different uses, according to the varying tastes and ages of different nations: and thus to them...

Whether this author was named **John** or **Cerinthus** <note>Cerinthus was an early Gnostic teacher; some early Christian critics argued he, rather than the Apostle John, wrote the Book of Revelation.</note>, and whether he wrote his book early or late, can be a matter of complete indifference to us: it is enough that he was thoroughly infected by that plague of prophecy <note>original: "Weissagungsseuche." However, certain mystical passages in the letters, as well as the introduction to the Gospel of **John**, make me inclined to believe that this apostle could indeed have also written the **Apocalypse**—but now to the matter itself.

# First Vision

...and from the speeches that he puts into his mouth, one must conclude that he imagined Jesus in that form. However, the entire vision on Patmos is fictional, and his interpretation of the image is an error into which his mystical philosophy led him—I intend to confirm this with reasons, and afterwards show what the image actually represents.

To be sure, I cannot exactly blame him for seeking to deceive his readers according to the customs of poets and prophets, and for giving himself the appearance as if these wondrous things had been revealed to him directly by God through supernatural apparitions. For if he had not followed the fashion and had not adorned his writing with miracles, his teachings would have found no approval among either Jews or Christians—who at that time, as is well known, were all waiting for miraculous events—but would instead have been received by them as cold-heartedly as some children receive the scolding sermons of an aunt in which Ruprecht <note>Knecht Ruprecht is a figure in German folklore who accompanies Saint Nicholas; he acts as a "bogeyman" who punishes naughty children. The author suggests that without the "threat" or "wonder" of miracles, the audience wouldn't pay attention.</note> does not appear. What is fair for one is surely reasonable for the other: and our contemporary scholars make use of the art of bringing "stale goods" <note>original: "verlegenen Kram," referring to outdated or unsellable merchandise, here used metaphorically for poor scholarship or recycled ideas.</note> to the customer, whether orally or in writing, only that today different delusions are in fashion than they were back then. 

In short, I do not wish to scold the author at all for the minor deception he played upon the world through his alleged apparitions of spirits—perhaps, however, he actually did often see wondrous things in his dreams, since his imagination was always occupied with so many strange images; and if this is the case, then he did not even knowingly deceive. But for the reproach I only just made against him—namely, that he was neither the first seer nor the first painter of the aforementioned image—Daniel <note>A reference to the Old Testament Book of Daniel, which contains apocalyptic visions of beasts and heavenly figures that predate and heavily influence the imagery in Revelation.</note> is also primarily to blame, among others,

## First Vision

...when he whipped the dove-sellers and money-changers from the public marketplace—which, after all, was located only in one corner of the Court of the Gentiles <note>The "Court of the Gentiles" was the outermost precinct of the Second Temple in Jerusalem, where non-Jews were permitted to enter and where commercial trade often took place.</note>—with such great vehemence, though this presumably occurred out of haste. Brazen feet <note>A reference to Revelation 1:15, where Christ's feet are described as "fine brass, as if burned in a furnace."</note> would not have suited him either; and even less could he, as a spirit, appear in a golden belt, since he never held this metal in particularly high regard. I also do not understand how he could now say he was the *Alpha* and *Omega* <note>The first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, used as a title for God or Christ to signify being the beginning and the end of all things.</note>, since he previously spoke so often of his heavenly Father, who is known to be the Father of all other humans as well. How could he, who knew he had a father, say he was the beginning and end of all created beings? In short, whether one views this image from the front or the back, one finds almost not a single brushstroke in it that has any relation to the particular characteristics of that extraordinary man who, because of his excellent *morals*, will certainly always remain worthy of veneration by all rational people. For these reasons taken together, I will be forgiven for asserting that the author [of Revelation] was entirely mistaken to view it as a representation of his crucified Master—now, however, I will state what it actually represents.

It is known that the ancient *Egyptians*, and with them *Moses*, their student <note>A common historical theory in the 18th century suggested that the Prophet Moses was trained in the secret wisdom and "hieroglyphics" of the Egyptian priesthood before leading the Israelites.</note>, believed—according to their creation story—that the *Globe* [Earth] came into being first, and shortly thereafter the *Sun*, the *Moon*, and all other *stars* arose from it. This was believed even though the *Sun* is many millions of times older than the *Globe*, and even though the upper planets—*Coelus* <note>Latin for "The Heavens." In the late 1700s, this was a name briefly used for the planet Uranus after its discovery in 1781.</note> or *Cybele* <note>A mother goddess often associated with the Earth or the planet Saturn's moons in older astronomical texts.</note>, *Saturn*, *Jupiter*, and *Mars*—were formed much earlier, while the lower planets—*Venus* and *Mercury*—on the other hand, were formed much later than the *Earth* with its *Moon*. These were cast out from the *Sun* like pumice stones and slag from raging *volcanoes*, as all those [can] easily...

# Revelation I. 10—18

<meta>catchword: hea-</meta>

will easily be admitted by those who have made themselves intimately acquainted with <term>mathematics</term> and <term>physics</term>. Furthermore, it is known that the oldest <term>geographers</term> only knew <term>Asia</term> and <term>Africa</term>, and perhaps did not even know both of those <term>continents</term> in their entirety; consequently, they only knew something of the three northern <term>zones</term>, while they believed the earth beyond the hot zone rested upon golden mountains. I also need not point out that in ancient times, the seven <term>geniuses</term> <note>original: "Geniuse". In ancient cosmology, these were guardian spirits or divine beings thought to govern the movements of the planets.</note>—by whom the seven <term>planets</term> were carried around the sky according to the customs and teachings of that time—were turned into the seven most noble spirits, or <term>archangels</term>, who stood before the Lord to carry out his commands. This means they constantly flew around the earth and directed not only all natural events but also the <term>fates</term> of mankind.

The green, <term>turquoise-colored</term> man <note>The author is likely referring to the description in Daniel 10:6, often linked to Revelation 1, where a figure's body is described as being like "beryl" (a gemstone that can be green or blue-green).</note> who is being discussed here thus signified the Earth, which back then, just as now, had a green appearance in most regions due to green herbs and trees. His light garment, which surrounded him, indicated the clouds or the <term>atmosphere</term>; and his glowing thighs had to represent the golden mountains upon which the earth was believed to rest in those days. The golden belt represented the hot zone, where the sun’s rays formerly set almost everything into a glow. The back of his face was the temperate zone, or the serene climate of the oldest <term>stargazers</term> <note>original: "Sternseher"</note> who first painted this image; while his snow-white hair signified the highest northern regions, where snow remains throughout summer and winter. The seven stars that he held in his hand were the seven planets, which were always tied to the earth, just as the seven lampstands—which were sometimes also painted as seven burning torches—indicated nothing more than the seven geniuses by whom the planets [were...]

# First Vision

carried around the heavens, and all events on Earth were set in motion <note>original: "angezeddelt," an archaic term suggesting something was plotted or instigated.</note>. But I cannot believe that a sword came out of his mouth: it was only a broad stream, resembling the wind-blast that painters would depict coming from the mouth of <term>Aeolus</term> <note>The Greek ruler of the winds in mythology.</note>, and which merely indicated the wind that the Earth—according to the delusion of some ancients—blew out of its belly, regardless of the fact that others placed the four <term>wind-gods</term> at the four corners of the world. Furthermore, as for his voice, which the author compares to the peal of a trumpet and the roaring of the sea: one can see without spectacles <note>A common idiom meaning "it is plainly visible."</note> that this signifies thunder, as well as the actual roaring of the sea. This also reveals at the same time that the author had not merely seen the image, but had read a description of it or had been orally instructed about it: for no painter can paint a voice—he would have to represent it symbolically—and the strength of a man’s voice cannot be judged from a mere painting of him. Moreover, the reason this man was called only <term>Alpha and Omega</term> <note>The first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, used here to signify the totality of existence.</note>, the Beginning and the End, was because throughout the entire <term>Orient</term> <note>The "East," specifically referring to ancient Near Eastern cultures.</note>, the Earth was regarded not only as the mother but also as the grave of all created things. Finally, everyone knows that all ancient peoples placed <term>Orcus</term> <note>The Roman name for the underworld or the god of the dead.</note> beneath the Earth, because according to their insights, no sun shone there; rather, because of the severe cold, there was nothing but weeping and gnashing of teeth. For this reason, they quite correctly fabricated the idea that the keys to <term>Hell and Death</term> were kept in the custody of the man described.

Thus, this image was nothing more and nothing less than the old <term>astrological symbol of the Earth</term>, and—in regard to future events—it signified nothing.

<meta>This page continues the author’s rationalist critique of the Book of Revelation. Revelation I. 10 — 18

Since the author had already claimed that his Master had appeared to him in this form, he naturally had to provide the reasons for such an appearance. Therefore, he put into his mouth those exhortations to remain steadfast in faith, which he gave to the leaders of the seven churches in the following two chapters of his book. These show sufficiently that he was either himself initiated into some kind of ancient <term>Mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites or societies, such as those of Eleusis or Egypt, which were a popular subject for 18th-century rationalist critics who sought to explain biblical miracles as misunderstood ritual practices.</note>—which, as is well known, were in fashion long before Moses—or was at least familiar with those mystical turns of phrase used by <term>initiates</term> <note>original: "Eingeweihete," referring to those who have been formally admitted into a secret society or mystery cult.</note>, though he did not understand their true meaning.

Thus he announced to the leaders of the churches in Ephesus, Smyrna, and Pergamon <note>original: "Pergamo"</note> what the image commanded to be said:

> To him who overcomes and remains faithful to me until the end, I will give to eat from the Tree of Life, which is in the paradise of God—he shall not taste the second death—I will feed him with the hidden manna and give him a good testimony <note>The German "Zeugniß" (testimony/certificate) here refers to the "white stone" mentioned in Revelation 2:17.</note>, wherein his new name shall be written, a name that no one will be able to read except for he who receives it.

Now, in the language of the <term>initiates</term>, these turns of phrase meant nothing more than: Whoever steadfastly endures the trials that the <term>Order</term> <note>The author is likely projecting the structure of 18th-century secret societies, like the Freemasons, back onto the early Christian context, suggesting the "seven churches" were actually local chapters of a secret society.</note> imposes on its candidates, and allows himself to be deterred by no suffering, no danger, nor even by death—which he must suffer figuratively, and consequently also be figuratively born anew, if he wishes to reach us—to him we give a new name, which he shall bear in our assemblies, and which no profane person <note>original: "Profaner," referring to those outside the initiation or the uninitiated public.</note> can read.

One should also not forget the observation that the secrets of the **Knights Templar** probably had many similarities to those **mysteries** of the ancients, if they were not indeed the very same: for the members of this order also all represented themselves as pillars of their temple, which they had claimed as their own property and chosen as an eternal inheritance—but this is only mentioned in passing.

### Second Vision

After the ancient stargazers <note>The author uses "Sternseher" (star-seers), a term that bridges the gap between ancient astrology and early astronomy.</note> had advanced somewhat in the sciences and already knew that the earth was round like a sphere, they believed that it stood in the middle of the cosmic structure <note>Original: *Weltgebäude*, referring to the universe or the "world-system."</note>. They took the heavens to be a spherical crystal vault—a hollow, blue sea of glass—to which the stars were fixed all around, facing the earth. 

Thus, when they wanted to represent the cosmic structure, they painted the Earth in the middle of a tablet in the form of a **green man** who had a rainbow around his head and sat upon a throne <note>Original: *Stuhl* (chair). In the context of Revelation and this astronomical map, it refers to the seat of authority or the central point of the cosmos.</note>. Not far from the throne, they placed the four constellations of the **zodiac** <note>Original: *Thierkreis*, literally "animal circle."</note>: the **Bull**, the **Lion**, the **Scorpion**, and the **Water-Bearer**. These were the most notable constellations of the entire sky at that time because within them lay the points of the **solstices** <note>Original: *Sonnenwenden*</note> and **equinoxes** <note>Original: *Nachtgleichen*</note>, as has been shown in the introduction to this present work. 

Outside the zodiac, the **Chaldeans** and **Persians**—as **Diodorus Siculus** <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE; the author is citing him to provide a historical, secular basis for the imagery in Revelation.</note> reports—counted another twenty-four constellations in the sky. Twelve of these, which according to their legend never set but always stood in the northern sky above the horizon, were at that time the lords and rulers of the day, or of the living. The other twelve, however, which were located in the southern hemisphere of the sky below the horizon and never rose but always remained invisible, had command only over the night and over the dead. But all twenty-four taken together were the judges of all mankind, of the dead and the living, and were likewise placed around the earth. Finally, they also led the sky itself around the entire painting in the form of a blue circular wall. Also we-

# Revelation IV. 1—8

...not to forget the seven **genii** <note>Original: *Geniusse*. In ancient cosmology, "genii" or spirits were often associated with the seven known planets.</note> of the planets, which they placed in the form of seven burning torches around the Earth—that is, around the man who sat upon the throne. But the throne itself meant nothing more than the **rest** <note>The author is highlighting the geocentric view where the Earth remains stationary (at "rest") while the heavens move.</note> of the Earth, which, as is well known, was believed in ancient times to sit entirely still, while letting the whole sky and all the images of the zodiac—which therefore had no rest day or night—wander incessantly around it.

Thus, the images of this supposed second vision were nothing more than the **astrological symbols** of the heavens and the terrestrial globe, and in regard to the future, they also mean—nothing.

The **green man**, who sat on the throne and had a rainbow around his head, was therefore once again the ancient symbol of the Earth. However, here he stood in the middle of the hollow celestial sphere, which consequently gave our author—or at least the older prophets from whom he presumably borrowed the entire **fable** <note>Original: *Mährchen*. The author uses this word to signal his skeptical, rationalist view that the vision is a borrowed myth rather than divine revelation.</note>—reason to place the scene of the appearance now in the sky. Now, it is indeed said that the body of this man resembled <term>jasper</term> and <term>sardonyx</term> <note>Jasper and sardonyx are varieties of chalcedony; jasper is usually opaque and multi-colored, while sardonyx has parallel bands of brown and white.</note> in its colors, while the rainbow resembled <term>emerald</term>. However, this report has turned out a bit incorrectly, whether due to the copyists, the author himself, or finally his sources. Properly, it should say: "His body resembled **emerald**, and over him stood a **rainbow** in which all colors could be seen, as in the most beautiful jasper and sardonyx." For no rainbow is merely green, but rather red, yellow, green, blue, and violet—and almost all these colors are also found in the two aforementioned types of stone when they are quite beautiful, especially the well-known **ribbon jasper**. But the body of the—

<meta>This page continues the rationalist interpretation of Revelation 4. It concludes the description of the "man" on the throne (representing the Earth) and begins a detailed astronomical explanation of the "four living creatures" as signs of the zodiac and the "seven torches" as planetary spirits.</meta>

...of the **man** <note>Referring back to the figure on the throne from the previous page, whom the author identifies as a personification of the Earth.</note> had to be painted green, as Daniel indeed says that he appeared blue-green or turquoise-colored; for the habitable part of the Earth’s surface is, after all, mostly green.

The **four beasts**, however, that stood around the man and around the throne, were the four constellations of the **zodiac**, the Lion, the Bull, the Water-bearer, and the Scorpion. The latter was mistaken by unastronomical stargazers for a flying eagle because of its outspread claws and many feet; in their opinion, such an evil creature as the scorpion could by no means have entered heaven. They did not have six wings either, but only four, and two hands each, which the author <note>The author of the Book of Revelation.</note> however, mistook for wings. The four wings indicated that their flight was directed toward all four corners of the world. The two hands, on the other hand, meant nothing more than the help which, according to the delusion of the ancients, they provided to mankind. They were also full of eyes inside and out; that is, they were covered with stars everywhere, because the constellations themselves consist of nothing but stars. For this reason, they could not rest day or night, because they have always seemed to fly around the sky day and night, and to this day they still seem to move restlessly around the earthly globe once every day.

But the **seven burning candles** <note>Often translated as "lamps" or "torches" in Revelation 4:5.</note> that stood before the green man and his throne indicated the **seven highest spirits**, by which people believed the seven planets were carried around the sky, and which were turned by the ancient Persians into the seven archangels who stood before the Lord.

<meta>This page continues the rationalist and astronomical interpretation of Revelation 4. ### Revelation IV. 1—8

The **twenty-four elders**, who sat in a wide circle around the seat of the Earth and were clothed in white garments, represented the aforementioned twenty-four constellations. According to the delusions of the **stargazers of Babylon** <note>The author attributes the imagery of Revelation to Babylonian astrology, which influenced Jewish thought during and after the Exile.</note>, these were the supreme judges of mankind; because of their love of justice, they wore not only golden crowns but also white garments. Black or multicolored clothing would certainly not have suited these figures, as they were supposed to consist entirely of stars and thus necessarily had to be enveloped in white light.

The **sea of glass** or **crystal**, which the author otherwise claims to have seen before the throne, was nothing more than the **sky**, which the ancient stargazers universally represented as a blue firmament <term>firmament: from the German "Veste," referring to the ancient belief in a solid, hammered-out dome over the Earth</term>—a crystal vault.

Finally, regarding the **lightning** that came from the throne, accompanied by peals of thunder as well as other loud noises: it becomes clear once again that these were merely intended to be understood as the thunderstorms and gale-force winds that often occur. It also shows that the author was instructed about these images either orally or in writing; for through mere sight, he could perceive neither the thunder nor the voices.

This latter conclusion will also hold true for many subsequent visions; therefore, from now on, I will always take it as a given and not constantly speak of it.

From the inverted order in which the author named the **four beasts**, it is evidently clear that either he, or his teacher, did not [proceed] like the **Hebrews**

## Second Vision

[The] man's <note>Continuing from the previous page, the author is describing the central figure on the throne as a personification of the Earth or a deity reflecting the Earth's nature.</note> figure had to be painted green, just as Daniel actually says that he appeared blue-green or turquoise-colored <note>original: "türkißfarbig." This likely refers to Daniel 10:6, where a vision is described as having a body like "beryl" or "chrysolite."</note>; for the habitable part of the Earth's surface is, after all, mostly green.

But the **four beasts** <note>Also known as the "four living creatures" in Revelation 4:6-8.</note> that stood around the Man and the throne were the four constellations of the **zodiac**, the Lion, the Bull, the Waterman <note>Aquarius</note>, and the Scorpion. The latter was viewed as a **flying eagle** by the unscientific stargazers because of its outspread claws and many feet, and because, in their opinion, such an evil animal as the scorpion could by no means have entered heaven. Nor did they have six wings, but only four, and two hands each, which the author <note>Referring to the author of the Book of Revelation</note> mistook for wings. The four wings indicated that their flight was directed toward all four corners of the world. The two hands, on the other hand, meant nothing more than the assistance they provided to mankind, according to the delusions of the ancients. They were also full of **eyes** inside and out; that is to say, they were covered everywhere with **stars**, because the constellations themselves consist entirely of stars. For this reason, they could not rest day or night, because they have always seemed to fly around the heavens day and night, and to this day still seem to move restlessly around the globe once every day.

But the **seven burning lamps** <note>original: "Kerzen" (literally candles, but here referring to the lamps of the Menorah imagery in Revelation).</note> that stood before the green Man and his throne represented the **seven supreme spirits**, who were believed to carry the **seven planets** around the heavens, and who were transformed by the ancient **Persians** into the **seven archangels** standing before the Lord.

<header>Revelation IV. 1–8</header>

Because of their love of justice, they wore not only **golden crowns** but also **white garments**. Black and colorful clothes would certainly not have suited these figures, which were supposed to consist of nothing but stars, and thus necessarily had to be shrouded in white light.

The **sea of glass or crystal**, which the author claims to have seen before the throne, was nothing more than the **sky**, which the ancient stargazers consistently turned into a "blue vest" <note>original: "blauen Weste." This is a metaphorical way of describing the atmosphere or the celestial canopy.</note> or a **crystal vault**.

Regarding the **lightnings** that proceeded from the throne, accompanied by **claps of thunder** as well as other loud noises: it is once again evident that these were simply to be understood as the thunderstorms and gale-force winds that often occur. It also shows that the author had been instructed about these images either orally or in writing; for through mere sight alone, he could perceive neither the thunder nor the voices.

This latter conclusion will also apply to many of the following visions; therefore, from now on, I will always take it as a given and not always speak of it.

<meta>This page continues the interpretive exploration of celestial visions. # Second Vision

Once, the stargazers <note>original: "Sternseher." Historically, these were observers of the heavens who combined what we now call astronomy and astrology.</note> observed a very large Northern Light <note>original: "Nordlicht." Also known as the Aurora Borealis; this passage suggests that ancient descriptions of divine glory may have been inspired by this atmospheric phenomenon.</note>, which traveled from the North in the form of broad fiery rays and reached all the way to the Zodiac <note>original: "Thierkreis," literally the "animal circle."</note>. These fiery rays were red and left large white spaces empty between them, which hardly obscured the light of the stars standing behind them at all. This Northern Light was followed by rainy weather, and this in turn by a rainbow with sunshine. Therefore, in order to leave a record for their descendants of this atmospheric phenomenon—which was perhaps rare to them—they first marked the four regions of the world on a tablet and then painted in a large fiery cloud that came up from the North. In the middle of this cloud, they left a bright white space to indicate the white spots in which the stars appeared. However, so that one would also know that this fire-cloud reached up to the Zodiac, they painted the most prominent animals of the same—the animals of the solstices and equinoxes: the Bull, the Lion, the Scorpion, and the Water-bearer—onto the aforementioned white space. But in the South, East, and West of the tablet, they painted a wide-spread sea, which represented the part of the sky that the Northern Light left clear. Finally, to indicate the rain that followed this phenomenon, they placed the Sun in the highest place of heaven in the form of a man on a blue chair <note>This "man on a blue chair" mirrors the description of God on a sapphire throne in Ezekiel 1 and Revelation 4.</note>, and let rays go out from him that descended into the clouds and formed all the colors of the rainbow.

Long afterward, an astronomer who is said to have been named Atlas invented the armillary sphere <term>Ringsphäre|A model of objects in the sky consisting of a spherical framework of rings, centered on Earth or the Sun, used to represent lines of celestial longitude and latitude.</term> of the heavens, which as is well known consisted of four hoops of equal size pushed into one another. Two of these formed the four colures <term>Koluren|Two principal meridians of the celestial sphere that intersect at the poles; one passes through the equinoxes and the other through the solstices.</term>, one the Equator, and one the solar path <note>The ecliptic, or the apparent path the Sun follows across the sky over a year.</note>, upon which latter the twelve constellations of the Zodiac,

# Revelation IV. 1—8

...or at least the four beasts of the solstices and equinoxes, were indicated. The hoops were painted green or blue and adorned with stars, because they represented the heavens, which shine everywhere with stars.

All these images and inventions later came to the keepers of the ancient mysteries, to the priests and stargazers <note>original: "Sternseher." In this historical context, this refers to those who observed the heavens before the modern scientific distinction between astronomy and astrology was made.</note> of that time in Babylon, Memphis, Thebes, and so forth. These keepers—perhaps out of jealousy, but perhaps also often out of ignorance—never explained their true meaning and use to any layperson <note>original: "Profanen." Those not initiated into the secret religious or scientific knowledge of the priesthood.</note>. Instead, they only hinted that the great secrets of the gods, and even the future fates of the world itself and of all humanity, lay hidden within them. Ezekiel, too, believed this; he probably had been able to snatch nothing more from their hieroglyphs <note>The author uses "hieroglyphs" here to refer generally to symbolic or sacred imagery used by ancient priesthoods.</note> than the aforementioned images. He then combined these and presented them as images of the Glory of God that had appeared to him, in order to give himself the reputation of a great prophet among his superstitious nation, who at that time thought themselves to be almost more than kings.

Thus, that fiery cloud coming from the North was nothing more than a Northern Light <note>original: "Nordlicht." Also known as the Aurora Borealis.</note>, and those four beasts appearing within it were nothing more than the four beasts of the solstices and equinoxes <note>The "four beasts" in Ezekiel and Revelation are often associated with the zodiac signs of the Bull (Taurus), the Lion (Leo), the Eagle (Scorpio), and the Man (Aquarius).</note>. These have indeed moved straight forward from the beginning and still move straight ahead daily from East to West. At the same time, however, within twenty-six thousand years, they also appear to run once from West to East, as well as a good distance from North to South and South to North, without needing to turn around <note>This is a remarkably accurate reference to the "precession of the equinoxes," a cycle that takes approximately 25,800 years to complete.</note>. They were given round feet because the path they travel is always circular. But the burning torches that moved between them,

# Second Vision

...those, signified the stars of the other constellations of the Zodiac, which move between them. The shining golden stars, however, with which the beasts themselves were spotted, and which appeared to the prophet like glowing coals, represented the stars of the four beasts <note>In Ezekiel's vision, these are the "four living creatures." The author identifies them here as the cardinal constellations of the zodiac.</note> themselves. Regarding the lightning <note>original: "Bliß," an archaic spelling of *Blitz*.</note> that came from the brilliance of the burning torches: this signified either actual lightning, or merely so-called shooting stars, which could indeed be viewed by some peoples as a special divine emanation.

The large turquoise-colored wheel, which appeared to be manifold and was set with eyes on its rims both inside and out, indicated the ring-sphere <term>armillary sphere: an ancient astronomical instrument composed of graduated metal rings representing the great circles of the celestial sphere</term> of Atlas. The prophet certainly had to form a very grand concept of this, because he had heard or read that these hoops were nothing less than the hoops of heaven, and moved entirely as the heavens themselves, or like the aforementioned four beasts. Certainly, in ancient times, there were uncommonly large astronomical instruments of this kind: for this is evident from the account of that gilded ring-sphere of King Osymandyas <note>The Greek name for Pharaoh Ramesses II; the author refers to a legendary golden circle described by the historian Diodorus Siculus.</note>, which was said to have been three hundred and sixty-five cubits in circumference and was once found in the funerary temple beneath a pyramid near Thebes. If the prophet knew nothing of these movable heavenly hoops, and of the four beasts that were painted onto the path of the sun <note>original: "Sonnenbahn." The ecliptic, or the path the sun appears to follow through the zodiac constellations over the course of a year.</note>, then he also could not have said that the beasts moved and rested like the wheels, and the wheels like the beasts: for in the heavens themselves, the aforementioned four beasts never rest; on the artificial ring-sphere, however, they must of course always rest whenever it rests, and move whenever it is moved or turned. ...the living wind, which was said to have been in the wheels, likely signified only the hidden powers of the gods, who moved all the heavens—and consequently also all these hoops <note>Referring to the rings of the armillary sphere discussed on the previous page.</note>—by their breath, according to ancient custom.

That finally the man who sat high in the heavens upon a sapphire throne, and spread rays of light around himself which played in all sorts of rainbow colors, was nothing more than the ancient symbol of the sun; as well as that either the prophet himself, or his sources, merely invented that rushing sound of the beasts, as well as the thunder, simply to give the image a more beautiful prophetic form—this I probably do not need to state first, as the matter is clear enough in itself.

Regarding the four beasts, it should further be noted that each of them here had two hands and four wings, whereas in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note> they consistently had six wings and no hands at all. One might thus be troubled by the metamorphosis of the two hands into two wings, or the two wings into two hands; however, how easy must it have been in those ancient images—which were undoubtedly very poorly painted—to confuse wings with arms, or arms with wings?

It is certain, however, that Moses borrowed the strange figures of the golden cherubim, which Bezalel <note>The craftsman appointed by God to build the Tabernacle and its furnishings (Exodus 31).</note> placed upon the Ark of the Covenant, from the images of these celestial bodies <note>original: "Gestirne"</note>, which had been given wings long before his time; for otherwise they could not have flown around the heavens.

### Second Vision

...follow, that the star-gazers <note>The author uses "Sternseher" to refer to ancient astronomers or astrologers.</note> who first painted these images had long known of the **precession of the equinoxes**, and consequently the **Great Year** <note>The Platonic Year, a period of roughly 25,800 years in which the equinoxes complete a full cycle through the zodiac.</note>, and had divided it into four equal parts, or **Ages of the World**, which may have been primarily subject to these four beasts. One can truly imagine no more sublime images of the veneration of God's majesty than these, and it will be easily seen that I have no objection at all to the imitation of them, if the veneration...

# First Continuation of the Second Vision

...had no relation to future events: but now our author begins to unpack his prophetic wares <note>original: "prophetischen Kram"; the author uses this somewhat dismissive term to suggest that the upcoming prophetic interpretations are elaborate or perhaps overly ornate constructions</note>, and to show us the images which the unfortunate intellect of fantastic astrologers created from the <term>hieroglyphs</term> <note>referring here to the symbolic shapes and characters of the constellations</note> of those most ancient star-gazers.

They, the astrologers, had namely observed that rain, heat, cold, fertility, crop failure, disease, and so forth, usually alternated in proportion to the various positions of the two great world-lights <note>the sun and the moon</note>: and now they concluded that the stars breathed their exhalations into the atmosphere of the earth, that all plants were impregnated by this breath, that humans themselves inhaled it, and that consequently all events on earth were dependent on the stars. But this <term>astral influence</term> <note>the theory that celestial bodies exert a physical force or "effluvium" upon terrestrial life</note> exerted, in their opinion, completely different effects during different constellations, and for this reason they further claimed that although all our encounters and destinies were written in the heavens, as if in a book, they could not easily be read by anyone because the letters had no definite shape.

This book was thus the great Book of the World, in which the fates of all humans were recorded from the very beginning, and which was given into the hand of the earth because it contained her own affairs—It is also called the Book of Destiny, of <term>Fate</term> <note>original: "Fatums," from the Latin *fatum*, referring to that which is ordained or spoken by the divine</note>, of inevitable destiny.

Even though the star-gazers knew well that the art of predicting future events from the constellations was a very precarious thing, and just as unreliable as the art of our <term>physiognomists</term> <note>practitioners of physiognomy, the study of a person's facial features or body as an indication of character or destiny</note>, who [read] in the hands, or on the noses, and upon the foreheads

...to spy out all the secret hiding places of the hearts of men: yet they by no means gave up the hope of gradually bringing their art to some level of perfection. Since, in their opinion, everything in the world depended on the constellations, they concluded that one day, with that general <term>conjunction</term> <note>an alignment of celestial bodies</note> of the planets, the present world would come to an end, and then all people, all animals, all events, all trees, yes, all thoughts, and in general all things, would return again in the very same order, or in the very same connection with other things, in which they had existed before <note>This refers to the "Great Year" or "Eternal Return," a philosophical concept where the universe repeats itself infinitely</note>.

It could also not be unknown to them that the ancient <term>Chaldeans</term> <note>an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their early developments in astronomy and astrology</note> had begun counting the signs of the <term>zodiac</term> <note>original: "Thierkreis"</note> starting with <term>Aries</term> <note>the Ram</note>: and from this they concluded that the sun and all the planets had stood together in this constellation immediately after creation, and consequently would one day stand together in it again,

## First Continuation of the Second Vision

...when they completed the entire sequence of their constellations in order to begin them anew. Because of this, this animal <note>The Ram, or Aries, which traditionally marks the beginning of the zodiac</note> became so highly esteemed that not only did the Egyptians show divine honor to its horns even before the time of Moses—placing them on the head of Hammon <note>Also known as Amun or Ammon, an Egyptian deity often depicted with the horns of a ram</note> or Ham, the brother of Japheth and Shem—but the Hebrews also ate the Passover lamb for its sake during their exodus from Egypt. Initially, it was depicted simply as a man with ram’s horns; however, the astrologers, who now enriched their hieroglyphic treasury with it and incorporated it into their mysteries, designated its prophetic meaning by means of a triple allegory as follows:

The ram itself was retained but presented as if killed, partly to note its annual <term>heliacal setting</term> <note>original: "heliakische Untergang"; the time when a star or constellation disappears into the sun's light at sunset</note>—for which they painted a wolf that had to strangle it—and partly to indicate through this strangulation the universal death that nature would suffer when all seven planets would gather again in this constellation. To indicate that the planets would indeed meet there in the future, this dead sheep was given seven eyes, which simultaneously signified the seven great spirits of the planets, the seven supreme gods, and the seven archangels. Finally, seven horns were placed on its head to show that all constellations ended after the seven orbital periods of the seven planets, and thus lasted for seven periods every time.

Each of these periods was later turned into a millennium <note>original: "Jahrtausende"</note>, partly out of ignorance of astronomy and partly because one happened to have ten fingers <note>The author suggests that the decimal system led humans to arbitrarily group years into units of ten or one thousand</note>. Thus it came to pass that the entire age of the world was given seven millennia, and each of these was ruled by one of the planets.

-># Revelation 5:1–8<-

Now this astrological fable also became known among the Rabbis <note>Jewish scholars and teachers of the law</note>, who, as is well known, have God create the world in six days according to Moses, and let him rest from his work on the seventh. These [the Rabbis] could not believe that the world would stand for seven millennia, and thus set its duration at only six: for according to their religious system, the seventh was the great Sabbath of the Lord, which our author transformed into the thousand-year reign of the faithful on Earth, or the famous <term>Millennial Kingdom</term> <note>original: "tausendjährige Reich"; the belief in a 1,000-year period of peace and holiness on earth</note>.

Thus, the book which he [John] saw in the hands of the ancient astrological symbol of the Earth was the book of destiny, or of the constellations. To be sure, no one could easily read this, because <term>astronomy</term> <note>original: "Sternkunde"; used here in the historical sense where astronomy and astrology were deeply intertwined</note> had fallen too far into decay; even our great modern astronomers would not be able to read it, despite being able to calculate all possible constellations to a hair. However, one has no reason to weep with our author over this human inability, since we would be no happier—but rather more unhappy—if we could calculate all our future encounters in advance.

The seven seals with which the book was sealed represented the seven astrological ages of the world <note>original: "Weltalter"</note>. According to the ancient view, the seven planets were said to finish the entire sequence of all their constellations at the end of these ages. From this, it is easy to understand that the events of each world age were sealed under each respective seal.

Now the author says that one of the twenty-four elders stated he should not weep, because the Lion of Judah would break the aforementioned seven seals. Nevertheless, it is not he who breaks them, but the Lamb.
